#149. Strait of Hormuz could reopen as part of Qatari plan for Iran nuclear inspections

Trump still considering new assault on Iran nuclear sites – though threats less credible after Vance remarks

Qatar, the lead mediator in the US-Iran conflict, is seeking to persuade Tehran to allow UN nuclear weapons inspectors to visit Iran’s bombed nuclear sites, including those housing the country’s stockpile of highly enriched uranium.

Iran would not be required to make any commitment that the stockpile would be removed from the country, with other options on the table, including reducing the uranium content by down-blending it.

Sheikh Tamim bin Hamad al-Thani, the emir of Qatar, spoke to Donald Trump about the proposal, the emir’s office said on Friday without revealing the call’s contents.

The state news agency said the emir “emphasised the importance of continuing dialogue and embracing diplomacy as a means of resolving disputes, preventing further escalation in the region”.

Trump is still considering whether to mount a further assault on Iranian nuclear sites, though the credibility of his threats is weaker inside Iran after his vice-president, JD Vance, said the war itself, and not just its cost to American consumers, was critical to the collapse of the Republican vote in the US midterms.

Under the Qatar proposal, the US would immediately lift the naval blockade on Iran in the strait of Hormuz and release a portion of the $8bn (£6bn) of its frozen assets. In return, Iran would agree to visits by International Atomic Energy Agency inspectors to its bombed nuclear facilities, based on a previous abandoned agreement that had been reached in Cairo in September 2025.

Iran would open the strait of Hormuz on the second day, via a route it has already negotiated with Oman that they would jointly lodge with the International Maritime Organisation (IMO).

Iran has multiple other preconditions for opening the strait including ending the war in Lebanon, a withdrawal of US forces from the Iranian coastline, a commitment to lift some economic sanctions, and agreement to pay war reparations. The sequencing of these commitments has been at the heart of the mediation.

In the memorandum of understanding, a $300bn reconstruction and development fund was to be set up to help Iran recover economically, but it was never clear exactly how this proposal was to be funded.

Iran, in a triumphalist mood after Trump’s election wipeout, has been wary of allowing the UN nuclear inspectors back into the country, claiming it had lost faith in Rafael Grossi, the head of the inspectorate.

Tehran claims Grossi betrayed his duty to uphold the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, by failing to criticise the US for bombing Iran’s nuclear sites. It fears that material gathered by the inspectors would be handed over as targeting data to the Americans and Israelis.

In the parallel talks on opening the strait, Oman’s foreign minister, Badr Albusaidi, spoke again to Iran about the future of the strait administration including the agreed route, the extent to which the agreement is temporary and the need to register the proposal with the IMO, either as a bilateral agreement or a joint regional proposal.

Oman has a long-term proposal for the administration of the strait compliant with the law of the sea in that shipping companies would be charged only for services, and not tolls.

Amir Hayat Moqaddam, the deputy chair of the Iranian parliament’s national security and foreign policy committee, told the Iranian Students’ News Agency (ISNA) on Tuesday that ships from four countries – China, India, Qatar and Iraq – were allowed to pass through the strategic waterway.

The exemptions served mutual interests, he said, adding that Iran charged Indian and Chinese vessels transit fees while allowing Qatari ships to pass free of charge. Medicine was received from India, while China provided political support.

It is reported that of the 10.23m barrels a day of Gulf crude that passed through the strait in October, about 42% went to China and 13% to India, said Muyu Xu, a senior crude oil analyst at the analytics firm Kpler.

The navy of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said two “violating” oil tankers attempting to pass through an unauthorised route in the eastern sector of the strait of Hormuz had collided with mines and caught fire.

The IRGC said the two tankers had turned off their navigation and identification systems and that the attempted crossing was with the support of US military forces.

#147. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#143. Court rules against Home Office in asylum seeker kidnapping case

Department had claimed that being kidnapped for ransom was not exploitation of trafficking victims in case of Syrian man abducted in Libya

The court of appeal has rejected claims by the Home Office that being kidnapped for ransom is not exploitation of trafficking victims.

The ruling means that many people who have been kidnapped for ransom, whom officials have previously excluded from being victims of exploitation under trafficking rules, will now have to have these claims considered. They may be eligible for the support and recovery package trafficking victims are entitled to.

Being kidnapped for ransom is a common hazard for asylum seekers passing through Libya on their journey to Europe. Many are fleeing conflict in countries such as Sudan and Eritrea as well as some Middle Eastern countries.

The legal challenge was brought by a Syrian asylum seeker who cannot be identified. The 25-year-old fled forced military conscription in July 2021, knowing he was likely to be imprisoned and tortured in Syria for refusing to fight.

He arrived in Libya, ultimately hoping to reach the UK. He and other Syrians were abducted by armed men and held in captivity for a month between August and September 2021. His money and phone were seized and they were driven through the desert for 24 hours, hearing gunshots, with no food and little water.

They were told they would not be released until their families agreed to pay a ransom of several thousand dollars. He was tied up and often beaten on his hands and feet by his kidnappers. He and the other captives had to phone their families to beg them to pay their kidnappers the ransom money. While they were on the phone, the kidnappers beat them so their families could hear screams. He was terrified that even if his family paid his captors, he might not be released and that his organs might be harvested.

The man was released after his family paid his kidnappers, and he made his way to Europe, crossing the Mediterranean from Libya to Italy by boat. He crossed the Channel from France to the UK on 16 May 2022 and claimed asylum.

The Home Office rejected his trafficking claim, arguing that because he had not been subjected to forced labour by his kidnappers, his experience did not meet the definition of exploitation in the trafficking rules. He challenged the decision in the high court, which ruled in his favour. The home secretary, Shabana Mahmood, appealed against the ruling.

While the government has pledged to support trafficking victims, the decision to exclude asylum seekers kidnapped for ransom in Libya has denied many the support the judges ruled they may be entitled to. Recent freedom of information data relating to asylum seekers who were confirmed to be genuine trafficking victims found that of the 1,778 confirmed victims between July 2024 and July 2025, 89% were forcibly removed from the UK, putting them at risk of being trafficked again.

While each trafficking claim must be considered on the facts of the individual case, the judgment found: “The secretary of state erred in not considering the question of whether kidnapping amounted to exploitation.”

The ruling could help thousands of potential trafficking victims, not only those who arrive in Britain in the future but also those previously rejected as being trafficking victims who were kidnapped and held to ransom in Libya. They can now get a fresh referral into the national referral mechanism for a new decision on whether there are reasonable grounds for them to be considered a victim of trafficking.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Jeremy Bloom, a consultant solicitor at Duncan Lewis, who represented the Syrian man, welcomed the ruling. “This judgment is a vindication of my client’s claim to be a victim of trafficking. The Home Office sought to restrict the definition of exploitation,” he said.

“We hope that it will also assist other victims of trafficking who have experienced similarly horrendous treatment to gain access to the leave and support that they need to recover from those experiences. Those individuals may include people who have had their claims to be a victim of trafficking refused, and who might now be able to ask for a new decision based on the law as set out in this judgment.”

Home Office sources said it would not be appropriate to comment on legal proceedings. A Home Office spokesperson said:“We are simplifying and strengthening protection and immigration routes, ensuring they are clear, targeted and focused on their intended purpose, while maintaining fairness and public confidence.”

#140. Israel’s president picks Yashar leader Gadi Eisenkot to form government

Knesset has been deadlocked since the election last month when Benjamin Netanyahu’s coalition lost its majority

Israel’s president has ⁠chosen Gadi Eisenkot, the prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s ⁠strongest rival, to ⁠try to ​form a new government, after a deadlocked election that left neither man ⁠with a clear path to power.

Isaac Herzog’s office said he had informed Eisenkot, the leader of the Yashar party, on Saturday night “of his intention to task him with forming a government”.

It added that the formal appointment would take place in an official ceremony on Sunday at the president’s residence in Jerusalem.

“Now is the time to form a national emergency government. I will do everything to ensure we are worthy,” Eisenkot wrote on X in his first reaction to the announcement of his designation.

Netanyahu ‌is fighting to hold on to office ‌after his religious-nationalist coalition lost its majority in an election on 27 October but still emerged as the largest bloc ‌in parliament.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

During party consultations with Herzog, 66-year-old Eisenkot received the endorsements of both Arab-majority lists, who could now play kingmakers after making unprecedented gains in the vote.

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot has laid out a series of conditions for any Arab party to join his coalition, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state and declaring the Palestinian militant group Hamas a terrorist organisation. One of Eisenkot’s partners, the more conservative Yisrael Beiteinu party, already has said it will not join a coalition with Arab-led parties.

Eisenkot, a former army chief, and his partners made equal military service a central part of their campaign. Specifically, they are seeking an end to the longstanding draft exemptions enjoyed by the ultra-Orthodox.

Netanyahu has repeatedly claimed the election was “stolen” by Arab parties, which rallied their supporters to vote in large numbers. But he has offered little to no evidence to support the claims.

Netanyahu is expected to continue his pressure tactics against Eisenkot, saying any alliance with the Arab parties would be an act of betrayal.

Israel’s Shin Bet security agency has launched an investigation into possible foreign interference in the vote, but the country’s Central Elections Committee – and Herzog – say the vote was valid and even if wrongdoing was found, it did not have a significant impact on the result.

Netanyahu called for a “national unity” government with Eisenkot on Saturday night but it remains unclear whether the two rivals could agree on a power-sharing agreement; these types of coalition governments are notoriously fragile and often collapse.

Eisenkot, the military chief of staff from 2015 to 2019, has set out to unite Israelis after Netanyahu’s divisive tenure, under which the country moved sharply to the right.

He has vowed to crack down on extremist West Bank settlers who have attacked Palestinians with impunity, decrying recent incidents as “Jewish terror” abetted by Netanyahu’s government.

But he opposes the creation of a Palestinian state and vowed to end Hamas’s rule in Gaza and destroy the Islamist movement’s military wing.

Commanding the military during the 2006 war with Hezbollah, Eisenkot devised the so-called “Dahiya doctrine”. The idea, named after a Beirut suburb heavily damaged in that war, involves Israel vowing to use massive force against civilian infrastructure in areas controlled by the Lebanese militant movement Hezbollah.

After his appointment, Eisenkot will have 28 days to come up with the 61 votes needed for a government. If he comes up short, Herzog can provide a 14-day extension before he has to offer the mandate to someone else.

If neither side succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections. The country held five elections between 2019 and 2022 owing to repeated deadlocks.

#139. Turkey pushes for transition at COP31 as coal burns

The host of this year's UN climate summit remains heavily dependent on fossil fuels as it tries to inspire a global switch to clean energy.

As Turkey welcomes delegates to the 2026 UN climate talks in Antalya and pushes for cuts in global greenhouse gas emissions, it can point to surging renewable energy capacity at home.

The COP31 host has approved more than 33 gigawatts (GW) of battery storage projects since 2022 that can power over 27 million homes.

Among the largest battery project pipelines in Europe, this storage will complement fast-expanding wind and solar energy that today provides 22% of electricity in Turkey, up from 4.5% a decade ago.

Yet this clean energy upswing is a long way from compensating for the country's overreliance on fossil fuels.

Turkey's coal-fired electricity generation increased by roughly one-third in the last decade, and in 2024 overtook Germany and Poland, which previously had the highest coal power capacity in Europe before it began to decline.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Turkey is among the few industrialized countries that has yet to commit to a phaseout date for coal — the fossil fuel with the highest planet-heating emissions.

Yet at the same time, "coal's position in the electricity market in Turkey is increasingly precarious," Efe Baysal, Turkey team lead at global environment network, 350.org, told DW.

He points to high local community opposition to coal projects on environmental grounds or due to lack of financial viability. According to German nonprofit climate campaigners, Beyond Fossil Fuels, some 97% of new coal projects have been cancelled in the last decade in Turkey, in what it calls "a global record."

"Turkey's coal project failure rate is the final nail in Europe's coal coffin," said Kasia Szeniawska of Beyond Fossil Fuels in a statement earlier this year. "Coal power is finished: it's financially risky, increases costs for billpayers, harms people's health, and sabotages climate goals."

But in June, Turkey announced plans to expand one of its largest coal plants and near double its capacity.

The move underlines Turkey's "conservative energy policy" and "highly insufficient" climate goals, said Umit Sahin, head of the climate change program at the Istanbul Policy Center, a global research institute.

Turkey's current climate target allows emissions to keep rising until 2038, meaning it will struggle to meet its plans to reach net zero by 2053, notes Climate Action Tracker, a climate research think tank.

Aside from climate concerns, Turkey's high dependence on imported fossil energy makes it extremely vulnerable to volatile global oil and gas markets. The nation currently imports 95% of its natural gas, 83% of crude oil and 60% of its coal — which together make up a "lion's share" of Turkey's energy capacity, said Baysal.

It is partly this dependence that has inspired Turkey's push for greater electrification at the COP31 climate conference.

In September, the country launched the "35x35" global electrification pledge, which aims to increase electrification of world economies from just over 20% today to 35% by 2035.

The goal is for electrification to replace fossil fuels, including in transport, buildings and industry, and preferably using clean energy, though this is not mandatory —heat pumps, for example, emit fewer greenhouse gases than gas boilers, even when the electricity is generated using fossil fuels.

The pledge complements existing UN climate goals of doubling energy efficiency and tripling renewables to keep temperature rise in line with the Paris climate agreement.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Turkey COP31 President Murat Kurum will promote the 35x35 pledge in Antalya on both climate and energy security grounds.

"[The war in the Gulf] has shown us, once again, that fossil fuels do not guarantee energy supply security," he said earlier this year at a UN climate meeting in Bonn, Germany. "Investment in alternative energy sources, in particular to support energy diversity, means stability, resilience and clean development."

But despite the rhetoric, Climate Action Tracker says that under Turkey's National Energy Plan, the COP31 host is only on track to electrify around 25% of total energy consumption by 2035.

Turkey is one of numerous Mediterranean countries facing extreme climate impacts like wildfires and record heat, in addition to recent severe and deadly flooding.

As it hosts this year's climate summit, the country has a great opportunity to hasten the energy transition, said Baysal, especially since it receives nearly double the annual sunlight of Germany.

Turkey is already starting to realize some of this potential, with one analysis showing the country produces more solar module manufacturing capacity than several major European countries combined.

Solar increased from around 0.1% of total installed electricity capacity in 2014 to almost 21% at the beginning of 2026, according to Turkey's resources and energy ministry.

The COP31 host can reduce its emissions 35% by 2035 if 10 GW of solar and wind is added each interim year — as opposed to the government's target of 8 GW — noted Umit Sahin. This goal will be helped by fast-expanding battery capacity, he said.

"But if you don't phase out coal, your emissions cannot go down," he added.

Meanwhile, the COP presidency could be a "turning point" and "a very important step for Turkey," said Sahin.

If the country wants to show climate leadership, and reduce energy dependency, it will need to embrace a "progressive climate policy" that promotes a full energy transition, he added.

Serdar Vardar and Jonathan Rampley-Sturgeon contributed reporting to this article.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

#136. Gadi Eisenkot reaches out to Netanyahu allies as he strives to form government

Former military chief says prime minister brought Israel to historic crisis and it is approaching ‘point of no return’

Israel’s former military chief Gadi Eisenkot started the tough task of forming a government on Sunday, reaching out to Benjamin Netanyahu’s allies as he said the veteran prime minister had brought the country to a historic crisis.

Eisenkot, 66, the leader of the Yashar party, who has vowed to bridge Israel’s deep polarisation under Netanyahu, formally received a mandate from the president, Isaac Herzog, who gave him 28 days to form a government despite his bloc lacking a clear majority.

“We are in a state of emergency, approaching a point of no return for the state of Israel,” said Eisenkot, who had traded his usual all black for a suit and tie at a ceremony at the presidential office. “We must form a national emergency government. This will require creative and unconventional political steps.”

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot and Herzog acknowledged Israel’s growing isolation under Netanyahu, who is wanted by the international criminal court.

In the US, Israel’s closest ally, Democrats on Tuesday swept the midterm elections, with calls to rein in Israel after the devastation in Gaza and the unpopular Iran war, which Netanyahu launched with Donald Trump.

Eisenkot said: “We are in the midst of a devastating diplomatic crisis. Even our closest friends and strategic allies are saying their relationship with Israel must be reassessed.”

He appealed to Netanyahu’s Likud party as well as the far-right party of the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, to join him – but only with himself as prime minister.

“My door is open and my hand is extended, wholeheartedly and willingly, to anyone who understands the gravity of this moment,” he said.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in the October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

#133. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#130. Why Nvidia CEO's affair with alleged Chinese spy matters

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has apologized for an affair with a woman the US believes was spying for China. The story could have profound implications for the world's most valuable company and the global AI race.

Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang's admission to an affair with a woman whom US intelligence officials believe was working as a spy for China could have profound implications for the $5 trillion (€4.47 trillion) company.

The Financial Times first reported on Huang's affair with Alice H., an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based customer of Nvidia, which has been suspected of trying to circumvent export controls to get advanced Nvidia chips into China.

The newspaper reported that the US government and its intelligence community believes she was recruited as a spy by China's Ministry of State Security intelligence agency.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

US government officials drafted a memo about the allegations shortly before US President Donald Trump traveled to Beijing for a summit with Xi Jinping in May.

Huang's relationship with the alleged Chinese spy has exposed him and the company to criticism that it may have allowed China to obtain valuable trade secrets as Beijing tries to gain an edge in the race to develop advanced AI models.

Nvidia is initiating an investigation led by independent board members and an external law firm. It said in a statement that Huang "has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort."

"The board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders," the company said Sunday. "While the board has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, the board's inquiry will examine all available information."

The company's shares fell in early trading on Monday.

While US intelligence officials have not commented publicly on the claims, Trump — with whom Jensen Huang has a close relationship — has backed the Nvidia boss.

"He's an honorable guy," said Trump. "I have not spoken to him ​about it. I just know he's a ​fine person, but you never know what is going on. I hope everything's fine."

Tim Culpan, an Asia tech reporter who has covered Nvidia and Huang closely for years, told DW that while Nvidia's fundamental strengths as a company remain in place, Huang is hugely personally damaged by the story.

"They're still going to make great chips and they're going to sell them to millions of customers around the world who want the best AI chips in the world," he said.

"His opinion is going to carry a lot less weight now. And that, in the long term, will flow through to Nvidia and Nvidia's own corporate interests. So it won't be immediate. It's really a fact that he doesn't have the sway or the power that he might have had one week ago simply through this."

Richard Windsor, a tech analyst and the founder of research company Radio Free Mobile, said the news could be very serious for Nvidia given how much the company has revolved around Huang for decades.

"This problem is particularly acute because Jensen Huang is very hands-on, meaning that the key decisions that led to its success were ultimately taken by him and because there is no obvious successor," he said.

"I suspect we are about to find out just how much the market thinks that the company is dependent on its iconic founder and CEO."

Nvidia and Huang's attitude toward China and potential Chinese customers has been a bone of contention with US intelligence officials — despite Huang's close relationship with Trump.

Huang has repeatedly lobbied Trump to ease restrictions on the sale of Nvidia chips to China. A 2025 export control deal heavily curbed sales, but Trump subsequently permitted the export of older H200 Nvidia chips to China in return for the US government receiving a cut of the sales.

However, that deal has been complicated and bogged down by Washington's strict export-licence review. Beijing has also initiated import controls, despite strong demand from Chinese companies. China lacks access to the most advanced AI-capable chips. Beijing wants to develop its own alternatives but has struggled to catch up.

Huang has argued that Chinese dependence on Nvidia's chips would prevent rivals such as Huawei from catching up and boosting China's ambitions to dominate AI supply chains.

However, that argument has been dismissed by many US intelligence officials, who fear that unlimited Chinese access to the most advanced Nvidia chips would boost their domestic AI sector, lead to their AI models catching up with the leading US models, and end up being used in Chinese military applications.

The US-China rivalry has become a central theme in the global AI story, with both countries seeking to gain an upper hand in the technology. Leading US AI executives such as Anthropic's Dario Amodei have argued that US AI companies can't afford to take a prolonged pause to allow safety standards in AI to catch up, if they're going to prevent Chinese firms from gaining an advantage.

Washington's central fear over Huang's affair is that it could have led to Beijing obtaining vital trade secrets or other key information about how Nvidia makes its most advanced chips.

Windsor thinks Huang will survive the "severe test" due to his importance to the company. He also cast doubt on the extent to which Beijing and its leading tech firms have learned from Nvidia's chips, pointing to China's own chip ambitions and commitment to have its firms buy its own semiconductorspointing to China's own chip ambitions and commitment to have its firms buy its own semiconductors.

"I am far from convinced that Chinese AI has materially benefited from technology or illicit chip imports that it gained from Nvidia," he said.

The news comes at a particularly challenging moment for Nvidia, which has benefited massively from the AI boom thanks to its position as the leading maker of cutting-edge semiconductors needed for AI applications.

Nvidia's valuation has soared dramatically in recent years as companies rushed to buy its chips, becoming the world's first $5 trillion company in October 2025.

However, doubts over the sustainability of the $9 trillion capital expenditure AI boom have sparked a major sell-off in stocks related to the boom in recent weeks. The Democrats' sweeping victories in the US midterms have added to the doubts, given the strength of voter opposition to data center construction in the US.

#129. French police officers on trial over beating of black man they accused of not wearing Covid mask

Assault on Michel Zecler in 2020 is rare case of police brutality reaching court, at time of heightened tensions

Three French police officers have gone on trial over the beating of a black music producer who they say was not wearing a face mask during the Covid pandemic, in a rare case of police brutality reaching court.

The assault on Michel Zecler, now 47, triggered outrage in France over police violence, which victims and researchers say is systemic but authorities and law enforcement unions claim is limited to isolated cases.

“I expect this trial to hold the police officers accountable,” Zecler told Agence France-Presse in a recent interview.

Campaigners and rights groups have said the case was further evidence of what they saw as the French police’s systemic racism, in a country where they said ethnic minorities received harsher treatment than white people.

Police brutality has been in the spotlight during the student-led protests in recent weeks, during which a 14-year-old lost most of his teeth when a police officer appeared to fire a grenade at him at close range.

Police stopped Zecler outside his Paris studio on 21 November 2020, accusing him of failing to wear a mandatory face mask and suspecting him of cannabis use.

They pushed him into the entrance of his office and violently beat him in an assault recorded by his studio’s security cameras.

Police officers Aurelien B, now aged 29, Pierre P, 37, and Philippe T face charges of intentional violence committed in a group, as well as forgery when they tried to cover up their actions in an official report. They face up to 15 years in jail.

The officers’ lawyers declined to comment before the trial, which started on Monday.

The producer sustained about 30 punches and truncheon blows to the face and body over a five-minute period, according to the investigation.

The medical report noted craniofacial trauma, a wound to the lip and scalp, and a complete rupture of his biceps tendon, leaving him unable to perform his job for 45 days.

France’s human rights ombudswoman has said police used “racist insults” during the assault, according to four witnesses. But the officers denied this and the alleged racism was not included in the indictment.

Zecler said they had used a racist slur against him. “I was insulted and I will repeat this at the hearing,” he said.

In their arrest report, the officers claimed the producer had hit them and tried to seize a truncheon, a version of events refuted by the camera footage that the French media website Loopsider released several days later.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

“I don’t understand the hatred I saw in their eyes,” said Zecler, who has a titanium anchor in his left arm after the ruptured tendon and says he still suffers nightmares.

“If there hadn’t been cameras … their reports would have been enough to send me to prison,” he said.

Most cases of alleged police brutality in France are dealt with internally.

A French police officer is to be tried over shooting dead 17-year-old Nahel Merzouk at point-blank range in June 2023 outside Paris. Video footage also contradicted the police’s version of events in that incident, sparking nationwide protests.

Guilty verdicts in alleged police brutality cases are rare.

In 2024, a judge gave suspended jail sentences to three officers who inflicted irreversible rectal injuries on a black man, Théo Luhaka, during a stop-and-search in 2017.

Young men “perceived as Arab, black or from north Africa” are four times more likely to be stopped by police than the rest of the population, according to a report last year by France’s rights ombudswoman.

They are 12 times more likely to be subjected to more severe measures, including being frisked during checks, it said after a survey of more than 5,000 people.

#128. AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Shares in artificial intelligence and technology firms have recovered slightly from an enormous sell-off that wiped trillions of dollars off stock values.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index closed in positive territory following a market rout that began in the US on Thursday before spilling over to Asian and European markets into Friday.

Investors had been selling off tech shares in part due to concerns the Democrat victory in the midterm elections would mean slower artificial intelligence development.

However the latest US employment figures added to a mixed picture for American markets on Friday morning, with an unexpectedly strong 165,000 jobs added to the economy in September.

On Thursday, businesses closely linked to artificial intelligence development took a hammering on stock markets, particularly chip and semiconductor makers.

But the Nasdaq Composite bounced back slightly on Friday, ending the day up 1.1%, partly reversing some of its 5.5% fall.

The S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly-traded US companies, closed up 0.3%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was flat through the day before ending slightly down 0.4%.

Companies that lost hundreds of billions in market value recovered some ground on Friday, including Nvidia, SpaceX and Tesla.

However, stock markets in Asia and Europe fared worse.

The Kospi in South Korea closed down 6% on Friday, with shares in chipmaker SK Hynix plunging more than 10%. European markets also fell, with the main indexes in Germany, France and Spain ending the day down.

In the UK, the FTSE 100, which tracks the largest companies on the London Stock Exchange, declined 1.01%.

In addition to the recent market volatility, UK traders were also digesting the latest Bank of England decision to raise interest rates and the signal that another rate rise could be on the cards to curb rising prices.

The market fluctuations also come after the sweeping Democrat victory in the midterm elections.

A number of Democrats campaigned for new rules and regulations for artificial intelligence (AI) and data centres, and the party has since won control of both houses of congress as well as key contests in states that have been part of the AI data centre race.

US President Donald Trump on Friday reiterated his supported for what he calls "super intelligence", saying only "foolish leadership" would "say no to the riches they bring".

However, there have also been concerns over whether the enormous amounts of money being spent on AI will pay off.

Russ Shaw, founder of global tech advocacy firm Tech London Advocates, said the election result was sending a message that a rethink was needed on AI.

"The incoming Congress is going to really focus on AI in a very dramatic way," he told the BBC's Business Today.

"I'm a big fan of AI, but I'm watching what these companies are doing, and they're not communicating effectively at all, particularly in the US, about what the benefits of AI are all about."

Pierre BaquÃ©, chief executive of AI platform Neural Concept, said the share movements were a "necessary market correction" that reflected a legitimate concern about the amount of money being poured into AI infrastructure.

"The industry must move beyond unchecked infrastructure investment and demonstrate greater financial discipline," he said.

"We are entering a critical transition phase, a market purgatory where raw tech must prove its widespread economic value."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said it had been a brutal week for markets, and the key question was whether the jump in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq would be sustained.

"Once investors have had the weekend to catch their breath, next week could give a clearer indication of whether the tech trade is going down the pan or fighting back."

Could the US midterms put a pin in the AI bubble?

Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

As well as the fallout of the midterms and the plunge in artificial intelligence stocks, US traders will also be digesting the latest jobs figures.

And those numbers were better than expected: US employers added 165,000 new jobs in October, well above the average monthly increase and more than double what analysts had expected.

Overall the unemployment rate, which counts the number of people who are out of work but actively looking for a job, eased slightly to 4.1% from 4.2% in September.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) said the unemployment rate has remained in the narrow range of 4.1% to 4.3% since March.

Neil Birrell, chief investment officer at investment firm Premier Miton, said while the jobs figures may feel like second billing to the market movements they were still important.

"The US economy is running hard which may mean the risks the markets are worrying about at the moment are not such a risk to the real economy as is being feared," he said.

#126. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

In an update this morning, the authorities said the 24-year-old Polish attacker, who had been injured in a shootout with police, has died in hospital.

Polish prosecutors had opened a terrorism investigation after the man, from the town of ‌Ostrowiec Swietokrzyski in ‌southeastern Poland, opened fire on Kraków’s popular Main Market Square on Saturday afternoon, killing six people, including a Briton, and injuring 17 others.

On the first of two days of national mourning declared after the rare mass shooting, police confirmed yesterday that three people found dead at the gunman’s home after the attack were his father, 53, mother, 52, and sister, 21.

The apparent European demand for at least some involvement in the discussions will stem from concerns that without their input Russia will leave with favourable terms.

This would significantly boost trade with the EU, but it would involve Britain accepting free movement rules. Norway is in this position – not in the EU, but in the single market.

At one point before the 2016 Brexit referendum, even Nigel Farage would sometimes say that the Norway option would be a good choice for the UK. But today Burnham is being advised to think twice before copying Norway – by the Norwegian PM.

double quotation markAfter Brexit, British friends called me and said, tell us about the Norwegian way of doing it. The two injured police officers – Jakub Kulik, a former paratrooper and volunteer firefighter, and Artur Morański– were hailed as heroes for their rapid response and for preventing further casualties.

At least 20 people have died over the weekend trying to reach Greece’s island of Crete, authorities confirmed earlier today.

Eight bodies were found on one boat and 12 on another, while an estimated 40 people were rescued off both, according to the Greek coast guard.

Greece receives a large number of undocumented migrants through the Mediterranean, often from nearby Turkey and countries in northern Africa.

Last month, Frontex, the EU’s border agency, said that the eastern Mediterranean was the busiest route for migrants attempting to enter the EU, although irregular border crossings into the bloc were down by a third so far this year.

Greece has been accused by human rights groups of forcibly returning migrants ⁠and ​asylum seekers on its sea and land borders, a ​practice it denies.

Many of the people making these dangerous journeys are trying to escape rapid climate change, famine, oppressive governments and war, and are often not eligible to apply for asylum from their home countries.

The US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, has said some progress had been made on what the “security infrastructure” of Ukraine and Europe could look like after the war with Russia.

Rubio’s comments on Sunday come as Russia-Ukraine negotiations under US mediation have been set for Tuesday and Wednesday in Abu Dhabi, with the talks expected to focus on postwar security arrangements, de-escalation, humanitarian efforts and steps to end the war.

US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, who is also Trump’s son-in-law, will represent the US during the talks. The Kremlin insisted this morning that no European ​officials will be there, rebuffing France’s foreign minister, Jean-Noël Barrot, who said French representatives would ‌be present.

In an interview ‌with CBS’s “60 Minutes” broadcast on Sunday, the US president, Donald Trump, said he believed the war would end ‌soon and suggested Ukraine could emerge from a settlement with altered borders. “I think we’re gonna have it done, and I think it’s gonna happen and they’re gonna end up with a little bit different shape Ukraine,” Trump said.

Despite Trump’s apparent optimism, no major breakthrough is expected at the talks as Vladimir Putin has consistently been sticking to his maximalist positions and both sides remain far apart, particularly on territory. But the US hopes the Russian president may be more willing to compromise after Trump struck ​a deal in October with the ⁠Kremlin to resume diesel exports to ​international markets.

A Ukrainian drone attack has hit Russia’s second-largest oil refinery in Kirishi, in the Leningrad region, the regional governor, Aleksandr Drozdenko, said.

A fire broke out at the refinery after the overnight Ukrainian drone strike, but has since been extinguished, he ​wrote on Telegram, adding that one ⁠woman had ​been killed ​by ​falling drone ​debris.

The Kirishi refinery, about 800km (500 miles) from the Ukrainian border, has suffered several Ukrainian drone attacks ​this year.

The facility, one of the key suppliers of diesel to ​the domestic market and for export, is reported to produce more than 6% of Russia’s total refined oil.

#121. Texas mayor instructs police to limit co-operation with ICE

John Whitmire is also calling on Congress to change immigration laws

The mayor of Houston, Texas, has directed the city's police to limit co-operation with federal immigration officials.

The move by John Whitmire, a Democrat, comes on the heels of stunning victories by the party in Texas during this week's midterm elections. It breaks with policy set by Republican Governor Greg Abbott, who lost his re-election bid.

"I do this to begin a healing process," Whitmire said on Friday, and to "reduce fear and anxiety across our community". He added: "I also do this knowing that elections matter."

There has been months of public outcry in Houston after an Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) officer fatally shot a man who was driving to work.

Houston police chief Noe Diaz said on Friday that officers will now only contact ICE if someone they arrest for a jailable offence also has an administrative ICE warrant, which are issued internally by the Department of Homeland Security (DHS), within which ICE operates.

"For example, if our police officers pull someone over for a minor traffic infraction and they write a citation, that person receives a citation and goes about their day," Diaz said.

Under the previous rules, Houston police officers were required to inform ICE if anyone they were dealing with came up as having one of these warrants attached to their name when they were searched on the National Crime Information Center (NCIC) - a digital database that includes records on wanted persons, stolen property, certain missing people and those arrested and fingerprinted for serious offences.

Whitmire said on Friday that he had directed the Houston Police Department to make the changes through an executive order.

He also called on Congress to change federal immigration rules, saying "ICE needs better training. They need body cameras. They need to be trained in de-escalation."

He added, "But this can only be done in Washington. I can only control what I can control."

Five ways Trump has changed immigration in the US

In response, a spokesperson for the DHS told Houston's NBC affiliate, KPRC 2, in a statement: "We remain disappointed that sanctuary politicians seek to limit cooperation with ICE, as our law enforcement are simply fulfilling their sworn duties to serve and protect our communities."

"Partnerships with law enforcement are critical to having the resources we need to arrest criminal illegal aliens across the country," the spokesperson added.

Another Texas city, Austin, has also begun implementing a similar policy change.

Democrats won both the US Senate and House of Representatives in Tuesday's midterms, taking control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Texas, a long-time Republican stronghold, was the site of one of the most stunning upsets in the vote, with the state electing its first Latina governor, Gina Hinojosa, as the first Democrat governor since 1995. It also elected its first Democratic senator since 1988.

Whitmire has previously faced criticism for abiding by immigration enforcement policies under the Trump administration.

He had resisted previous calls to implement similar changes over concerns that the White House could send a surge of immigration officials in response. , external

Abbott has also threatened to take pull millions in funds from cities like Houston, Dallas and Austin unless they allow police to cooperate fully with federal immigration officials.

The BBC has contacted Abbott's office for comment.

Whitmire's announcement comes a week after an ICE officer was charged with aggravated assault with a deadly weapon after he allegedly hit a man with a vehicle in Texas.

Earlier this year, large protests broke out in Houston after an ICE officer fatally shot a man driving to work.

The deceased, Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, a 52-year-old Mexican national who lived in the US as an undocumented immigrant for decades, was not the intended target, officials said after the incident.

#119. UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

Michel Barnier says there will be 'no cherry-picking' in a deal for the UK to returns to the EU

The prime minister has said rejoining the bloc's internal market was among options he could pursue as part of a reshaping of ties since the UK's exit from the EU nearly six years ago.

But Michel Barnier told the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg that EU members would insist on the UK readopting certain conditions, such as the free movement of people, to gain access.

He said there was not a "single market Ã  la carte" menu from which the UK could pick and choose what elements it wanted to adopt.

Barnier - who led the EU delegation during Brexit talks between 2016 and 2021, and had a three-month spell as French prime minister in 2024 - said the "door is open" within Europe to the UK rejoining as an EU member.

But he insisted that any move to rejoin the single market - which allows for goods to be freely traded without border controls - would require the UK to follow the same terms as other members.

He added that this included compliance with regulations designed to promote the circulation of goods, services, capital and people, referred to collectively within EU institutions as the "four freedoms".

"There will be no possibility to have one freedom and not the other. The four freedoms are clearly the four conditions to be inside a single market."

Since becoming prime minister in July, Burnham has suggested he could commit Labour to pursuing a much closer future relationship with Europe, including asking to rejoin the organisation as a full member, though he has ruled out major changes before the next election.

His predecessors have struck a series of deals with the EU on different aspects of how the UK and EU interact post-Brexit.

Both sides are hoping to use a meeting in Brussels in 10 days' time to unveil deals on food standards, youth visas and re-linking British industry to the EU's carbon trading regime.

Why the prime minister wants to talk about Brexit again

Of the four freedoms, the free movement of people - which allows EU citizens to live and work elsewhere in the bloc without a visa - was a key issue during the 2016 Brexit referendum.

Burnham's immediate predecessor, Sir Keir Starmer, ruled out re-establishing it for the UK in Labour's 2024 election manifesto and any move to advocate it again would prove a key dividing line with the Conservatives and Reform UK at a future poll.

Burnham recently said in an interview that he would not re-establish free movement of people without a referendum, prompting Downing Street to clarify that he was not advocating its return at this point.

He has previously said he wanted to lay out "different options" for the relationship with Europe at the Brussels summit later this month, before trying to find a consensus with the British public on a new relationship.

Barnier also predicted that EU countries would reject reinstating the British financial rebate, first negotiated in 1985, that lowered UK contributions towards the EU's annual budget while it was a member.

The UK's decision to leave the bloc had created "a new situation", he said, meaning a new rebate would be "off the table".

Barnier also compared Reform leader Nigel Farage to French politician Marine Le Pen. He said: "We have our own Farage. It is Mrs Le Pen. The same populism, the same lies, the same false promises."

Asked by Kuenssberg to clarify what he was referring to with his accusation of lies, Barnier said "for instance, when Mr Farage and Mr Johnson promised the UK people to get back [Â£350] million per week, it was not true, it was not possible. It was a lie".

The Vote Leave campaign, of which Boris Johnson became a key figure, has been criticised previously for the slogan on the side of their campaign bus that said Britain sent Â£350m a week to the EU, adding: "Let's fund our NHS instead".

Johnson said in 2017 that "once we have settled our accounts, we will take back control of roughly Â£350m per week. It would be a fine thing as many of us have pointed out if a lot of that money went on the NHS".

Critics have said that the Â£350m per week was an approximate sum which represents a "gross contribution" and did not take into account the UK's Â£75m-a-week rebate nor the EU's investments in the UK.

Johnson has since defended the claim, saying the Â£350m was based on the UK's gross contribution to the EU, and was in fact an underestimate as UK contributions were due to rise.

âIn his memoirs published in 2024, Johnson said EU money spent in the UK was not "democratically controlled by the British people", and "even if the net figure was only Â£175m, that was still a hell of a lot of money".

Farage, who led Leave UK and was not part of the Vote Leave campaign so not connected to the Â£350m claim, said: "It is the same old Barnier - arrogant, out of touch and a bully. He vindicates our decision to leave every time he opens his mouth."

#118. EU sues IBM and two European firms over troubled entry/exit border system

Lawsuit filed last year against IBM, Atos and Leonardo after delays to delivery to troubled new system

The EU has filed a lawsuit against IBM and two other companies involved in its troubled border-management system, an internal document has revealed.

The bloc’s IT agency, EU-Lisa, filed a case last year against IBM, the French IT company Atos and Italian defence contractor Leonardo, the three members of a consortium to implement and maintain the EU’s entry/exit system, which was launched in October 2025.

The rollout has faced significant difficulties: some passengers at peak times have missed their flights as a result of delays blamed on the new technology and a lack of staff at the border.

The agency’s decision to sue the companies was revealed in its latest annual accounts, which stated that “following the various delays in the delivery of the EES by a contracting consortium the agency activated the available contractual and legal remedies to protect its financial interest”.

Since its launch the system has been beset by problems. Airports have complained of malfunctioning EES kiosks to regular outages of the system, which have led to waiting times of three hours at peak periods.

EU-Lisa said a claim had been submitted on 22 December 2025 to a court in Estonia, where it has its headquarters.

The agency declined to comment on the details of the case, but said the proceedings concerned “the system’s development phase and have no impact on its current operation” adding that “EES is fully operational”.

The lawsuit was first reported by the Financial Times.

The European Commission said it was not a party to the case, while noting it concerned “delays in the delivery of the EES at the time of system development” and was “not related to the operation of the EES”.

The system increased security checks on passengers travelling into the EU from outside its border-free travel zone. Travellers’ faces have to be photographed and fingerprints scanned before they are allowed into the Schengen area, which comprises 25 of the 27 EU member states as well as Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway and Switzerland.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The checks apply to people who are not citizens of one of the 29 Schengen countries, Ireland or Cyprus.

The system is managed by EU-Lisa, the bloc’s agency for large-scale IT systems in the domain of justice and security.

The Guardian has contacted IBM and Leonardo. Atos declined to comment.
