#113. More than 400,000 Russians killed fighting in Ukraine war, leaked data suggests

Hacked death records reveal scale of losses since Moscow launched full-scale invasion nearly five years ago

Ukraine war briefing: post war ‘security guarantees’ a key focus in upcoming Abu Dhabi talks, says Rubio

More than 400,000 Russians have been killed fighting in Ukraine since Moscow launched its full-scale invasion nearly five years ago, according to leaked data from the country’s state statistics agency, Rosstat.

The data, obtained by the Belarusian opposition-linked hacker group Cyber Partisans and the Ukrainian hacking collective Silent Crow, contains more than 11 million death certificates, including details of the deceased’s age, place of residence and location of death.

It was later shared with the Guardian and a small group of other outlets.

Of those, according to the groups’ data, 412,576 death certificates explicitly linked the fatalities to the war, either through official cause-of-death codes or written references to combat and military operations.

The figures underscore the staggering human toll of Russia’s nearly five-year invasion of Ukraine, with no end to the war in sight despite a fresh round of peace talks scheduled to take place in Abu Dhabi this week.

The two hacking groups announced the breach on 1 November and published the data over the weekend. Rosstat confirmed it had been hacked but denied that any sensitive information had been leaked.

The dataset offers an unprecedentedly detailed picture of Russia’s wartime losses, which Moscow has sought to conceal. The Russian authorities have not published a death toll since the early months of the invasion, and information about military casualties is treated as a state secret.

The Guardian could not independently verify the authenticity of the entire dataset. However, the figures are broadly consistent with other estimates of Russian losses. British intelligence in September previously estimated that about 500,000 Russian soldiers had been killed since the start of the invasion.

Separately, the independent Russian outlet Mediazona has identified by name nearly 286,000 Russian military personnel killed in Ukraine, drawing on official records, social media obituaries and photographs of gravestones. The outlet estimates that the true death toll is closer to half a million.

“The data looks authentic, and it’s very rich”, said Janis Kluge, the head of the Russia and Eurasia division at the German Institute for International and Security Affairs.

“This is the biggest leak of data when it comes to Russian casualties,” Kluge, who has extensively studied Russian war statistics, added.

The dataset is likely to underestimate the true death toll. Some soldiers remain missing, while others have never been identified.

Kluge also pointed to a significant delay between deaths occurring and being entered into official records, which he said typically amounted to about six months.

“We are probably at this time closer to 500,000 killed Russian soldiers than 400,000,” he said.

The figures reveal the starkly unequal burden of the war across Russia, with poorer and more remote regions suffering disproportionately heavy losses.

In Buryatia, Tuva and Zabaykalsky Krai, three regions in eastern Russia, war-related fatalities accounted for about a third of all deaths among men aged 18 to 49 between the start of the invasion and mid-2024. In Moscow and St Petersburg, the equivalent figure was about 10%.

The leaked data also points to a sharp rise in Russian battlefield deaths as the war dragged on, with some of the deadliest months coming in late 2024 and 2025.

Kluge, who has been analysing the data, said the number of soldiers killed in several months during that period exceeded 15,000, equivalent to roughly 500 deaths a day.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

“In late 2024 and in 2025, you see that the numbers in many months are above 15,000 per month, and that’s higher than I think most estimates were,” Kluge said. “That’s 500 a day, above 500 a day. Certainly shocking.”

The scale of the losses is staggering by any measure and unprecedented in Russia’s modern history.

In less than five years, Russian military deaths in Ukraine are estimated to have exceeded the combined death toll from all Soviet and Russian wars between the end of the second world war and the start of the full-scale invasion in 2022 more than eightfold. The figure also exceeds the number of US military personnel who died during the second world war.

But rather than push Moscow towards a settlement, the scale of the losses is likely to harden Vladimir Putin’s resolve, according to a person familiar with the Russian president’s thinking.

“Putin understands these numbers, and for him to now settle for anything less than everything he wants would feel like a betrayal of himself and the people,” the person said.

Senior Russian and Ukrainian negotiators are due to meet in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday for the first US-brokered peace talks between the two countries in nine months.

Previous rounds of negotiations have made little progress towards ending the war, with talks stalling over Moscow’s demand that Ukraine withdraw from the parts of the Donbas region it still controls.

Neither Vladimir Putin nor Volodymyr Zelenskyy will attend the talks. Instead, both countries will send senior delegations, with Donald Trump’s envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner also expected to take part.

In a post on X, Witkoff said the negotiations would focus on “post-war security arrangement, de-escalation” and humanitarian issues.

European governments have expressed concern that the talks could extend beyond Ukraine to broader questions of European security, including Moscow’s longstanding demands for a reduction of Nato’s military presence in countries bordering Russia.

France’s foreign minister said Paris would participate in the negotiations but the Kremlin subsequently denied that France would be represented.

→ Baca artikel asli

#112. Meghan's father Thomas Markle under house arrest after child sex abuse material charge

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, has been placed under house detention at his assisted living facility in Florida after being charged over possession of child sex abuse material.

Markle, 82, was arrested in Florida on a federal complaint alleging he held images that show child sex abuse, the US Department of Justice (DoJ) said.

He is charged with one count of possessing indecent images of children, the DoJ said. Markle has previously denied allegations of possessing images of child sex abuse, calling them "sick lies".

In a 30-minute court hearing on Friday he was also ordered to submit to electronic monitoring, but no plea was taken.

At the court hearing, the charges were laid out against him by federal prosecutor Courtenay Derry, according to the BBC's US partner CBS News.

He was brought into court in a wheelchair, escorted by two US Marshals. Markle was wearing black trousers and a black t-shirt.

No plea was taken during the hearing, and an arraignment date in California will be determined in due course, a joint statement from the DoJ and the US Attorney's Office Central California said.

The Duke and Duchess of Sussex have not commented on Markle's arrest.

Markle was arrested after the FBI, acting on warrants, searched storage units in LA registered to him which he had rented since 2008, with the last payment being made in October this year, the DoJ said.

Inside the units the FBI says agents seized about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

FBI director Kash Patel said authorities "moved quickly" when arresting Markle.

"Mr Markle allegedly targeted innocent children and sought to steal their innocence through his criminal actions," Patel said.

He added that the FBI would "use every single resource at our disposal to protect innocent kids â any predator who targets children will have zero safe haven in this country or anywhere they try to hide".

Thomas Markle appeared in court on Friday

Witnesses "positively identified" Markle in two of the images believed to contain child sex abuse material.

In those images, the man pictured "appears to be in the age range of 30s to 40s", which suggests the alleged abuse dates back decades as Markle is currently 82 years old.

Last month, police in the Philippines obtained a warrant to examine devices allegedly removed from Markle's former home in the country.

Four boxes of material, including computers, phones, hard drives and old video tapes, were handed to officers by Meghan's half brother Thomas Markle Jr.

Markle Sr moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and lived in Cebu until May. He was then reportedly living in a Florida rehabilitation centre learning to walk again after having his leg amputated.

In October, London's Metropolitan Police confirmed that it had received a letter in 2019 making a "number of claims" relating to Markle Sr.

The Met said information contained in the letter, which related to allegations against Meghan's father outside the UK, was passed on to the office of the Duke and Duchess of Sussex, but it did not share it with other law enforcement agencies.

The force has since voluntarily referred itself to the Independent Office for Police Conduct (IPOC) over its handling of the letter.

In a statement released in October after the allegations against Markle Sr emerged, the Duchess of Sussex said: "I am deeply distressed at the news that has come to light in the last 24 hours, and my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

Meghan has been estranged from her father since 2018. The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year.

#109. KFC’s arrival at Italian ski slopes angers food and mountain purists

Fast-food outlets at two prestigious Dolomites resorts are Italy’s first, with legal action threatened

Skiers on the slopes of two of Italy’s most prestigious resorts will be able to dine from buckets of fried chicken between runs this winter as the US fast-food chain KFC prepares to open outlets, infuriating mountain and food purists while prompting a prominent businessman to threaten legal action.

KFC will open its first high-altitude restaurants on the slopes of Cortina d’Ampezzo and Madonna di Campiglio, in the Dolomites, on 5 December, an initiative the company said was aimed at “surprising people” and “reaching places” where nobody would expect to find it.

The move marks a debut for any fast-food outlet on an Italian ski slope, bringing a menu replete with KFC’s most popular products, from spicy wings and burgers to traditional buckets of fried chicken.

The plans appear to have taken people by surprise, not least Alberico Zardini, a majority shareholder in Faloria Spa, the company that manages the infrastructure on Mount Faloria in Cortina d’Ampezzo, where there is to be a KFC at 2,123 metres.

“The mere idea is disgraceful,” Zardini told Il Messaggero. “It’s absurd and shameful to imagine a fast-food joint in the most beautiful spot in Cortina. Do the people of Cortina not realise what we’re risking here?”

Zardini said he had written to the bosses of Faloria warning that he would pursue legal action unless they put the brakes on KFC’s mountain ambitions.

He said it was “a mad decision”, especially because the outlet would be selling fried chicken inside the Faloria mountain club, where a luxury lodge is due to open on an upper floor. “I am 100% against it and will not just sit back and watch,” he said.

The KFC in Madonna di Campiglio, in south-western Trentino on the opposite side of the Dolomites, will be at 1,700 metres on Piana di Nambino, next door to a swish après-ski club.

The two outlets came under the fast food chain’s “quick service restaurants” product range and were the company’s way of targeting a broader market beyond its loyal Gen Z customers, “from young families to mountain lovers”, said Marzia Farè, the chief marketing officer for KFC Italia.

“We strongly wanted this project because it perfectly represents the way we imagine the evolution of KFC: continuing to surprise, reaching places where no one would expect to find us, and doing so without giving up what makes us unique,” she told brandnews.it.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

Needless to say, with the cost of ski passes in Italy increasing each year, the outlets will appeal to those looking for cheap food in otherwise extremely pricey resorts.

But Oscar Farinetti, the founder of Eataly, Italy’s high-end food-store chain, has said the brand “doesn’t belong here at all” – while pointing the blame at customers. “We Italian consumers should be a tad more cultured,” he told Il Sole 24 Ore. “We need to acknowledge our assets – our cuisine made of few ingredients and excellent products. There is a lack of studying one’s own roots – we’re eating fried chicken instead of a nice slice of salami with a glass of fresh wine.”

Luigi Casanova from Mountain Wilderness, an NGO that works to protect mountain environments, told Corriere della Sera: “The era of polenta and luganega sausage is over,” while Paola Demagri, from Trentino’s provincial council, spoke about mountain identity being “sold out to Kentucky chicken”.

Others were more measured in their response. Cristian Ferrari, the president of Società Alpinisti Tridentini, the oldest mountain association in the Trentino area, said: “We have nothing against fried chicken, but are we sure this is the issue we should be most worried about?”

#108. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#107. US offers $20m reward for information about Iran's nuclear programme

The US has announced a reward of up to $20 million for information about Iran's nuclear programme.

The State Department's Rewards for Justice is also offering to potentially relocate the person who provides such details.

President Donald Trump claimed last year that Iran's nuclear facilities had been "completely and totally obliterated" following a bombing campaign by the US and Israel.

Iran insists its nuclear programme is peaceful and that it has no intention of developing a nuclear weapon.

Meanwhile, the US military says it is setting up a task force to enhance Washington's efforts to restrict Iran's movement of weapons and sanctioned material across its borders.

The US military's Central Command (Centcom) said Task Force Iron Gate would operate from Tampa, Florida, and would work with other US government agencies.

As well as the movement of weapons, it will also focus on dual-use and illegal revenue streams.

Centcom estimates that its naval blockade of Iran's ports has prevented the country from exporting more than $25bn in crude oil.

Major construction ongoing near Iran missile bases, satellite images suggest

A US State Department statement about its multi-million dollar reward said it was offering the money for information about the Iranian programme, including "facilities, equipment and associated personnel".

It said Iran's "lack of compliance with the IAEA [the International Atomic Energy Agency] on safeguards obligations has led to the agency's loss of 'continuity of knowledge' regarding Iran's production and inventory of key components like [nuclear] centrifuges".

Centrifuges are needed to produce enriched uranium, which can be used to make reactor fuel for civilian use but also for nuclear weapons if enriched to around 90% purity or higher.

There had been mounting speculation that the US might resume significant strikes in Iran after the midterm elections, though Trump told reporters, prior to the polls, that he was in "no rush" to do so.

In an interview with Fox News on the eve of the midterms, the president also said the US "might need to go after Pickaxe [Mountain]", an underground site linked to Iran's nuclear programme.

This was "not a big deal" and could be done in "one hour", Trump said.

The president has repeatedly threatened to bomb the suspected underground site on previous occasions.

However, both Trump and Vice-President JD Vance have said public opposition to the war on Iran fuelled heavy losses for their Republican Party in this week's midterm elections, which has led to speculation the White House may no longer seek an escalation in the conflict.

On Friday, Trump insisted that Iran was keen to end the eight-month conflict with the US, stressing the damage to Tehran's economy and military.

"We're dealing with them now. They want to make a deal very badly," he said.

Qatar's foreign ministry spokesman said on Tuesday that both sides were "reviewing the ideas and proposals we have presented" and that it remained hopeful of a deal being agreed.

Iranian negotiators had discussed with mediators a proposal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz within two days once the US met its seven conditions.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

#105. Russell Brand sentenced to over 10 years in prison for rape and sexual assault convictions

The former comedian was found guilty of raping one woman and sexually assaulting three others last week. The judge referred to Brand as a "predator."

British media personality Russell Brand was sentenced to 10 years and 6 months imprisonment on Monday after being found guilty of rape and assault.

He is required to spend at least two-thirds of that time in prison, and may be released under supervision after that if he displays good conduct while in jail.

Judge Joel Bennathan called Brand a "predator," and said that the sentence is "just and proportionate...to the overall gravity of your criminality."

Bennathan also praised the "determination and bravery" of the survivors who came forward.

The 51-year-old had consistently denied the charges, brought about by six victims on allegations going back as far as 1999.

A jury of four women and eight men did not find his defense that all the encounters were consensual to be plausible, and found him guilty of raping or sexually assaulting four of his accusers.

At the sentencing hearing, some of his victims gave victim impact statements to the court.

Another survivor called Brand a "sexual predator who has no remorse."

UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham hailed the bravery of the survivors in a statement, and said his government would be "relentless in making whatever improvements we can” to provide more support for victims and make sure every police station has a sexual assault response team.

Brand rose to prominence in the UK stand-up scene in the early 2000s with sets focused on his sex life, alcoholism, and heroin addiction.

He then became a presenter for several British television shows before starring in several Hollywood movies and being in a brief, high-profile marriage to American pop star Katy Perry.

As accusations of predatory behavior toward women began to mount, Brand left London and Hollywood behind for Florida. His politics changed from left-wing activism to promoting conservative ideas and conspiracy theories on his podcast.

He has said he underwent a religious conversion around this time. In 2016 he resumed his relationship with restauranteur Laura Gallacher, whom he first dated in 2007 when he was 31 and she was 19. They have since had three children together. Gallacher has remained by Brand's side throughout the court proceedings.

#103. CFMEU ‘enjoy a significant level of influence’ over Victorian government, Big Build royal commission hears

Inquiry into allegations of corruption begins block of hearings focused on 2022 level crossing removal project

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The royal commission into allegations of corruption on Victorian Big Build projects has heard a major construction company feared putting the Construction, Forestry and Maritime Employees Union (CFMEU) offside because of its close links to the Daniel Andrews Labor government.

The first block of hearings under the commission into the integrity of major public and civil infrastructure construction projects in Victoria will hear evidence about the union’s involvement in a level crossing removal project at Gap Road in Sunbury, north of Melbourne, in 2022.

The project involved the removal of a level crossing and the construction of a new rail bridge and underpass.

The counsel assisting the commission, Chris Young KC, said 20 witnesses have been compelled to appear during the November hearings.

But he warned that others were not complying with orders to produce documents to the commission, including using the excuse of “government policy” to delay their production. Failing to comply with such orders is a criminal offence.

“It is extremely concerning that some parties have not complied with the terms of those notices to produce,” Young said.

The hearings in November will focus on what government officials did during a June 2022 meeting in order to appease the union, who had forced work to stop on the Gap Road project.

Young showed the commission evidence that – in the years before the union interfered in the project – there was increasing awareness within the construction sector about the prospect of industrial action curtailing work, and the growing influence of the CFMEU.

He said that in 2017, one of the companies involved in the Gap Road project, John Holland, noted that the CFMEU was “proud of the fact that none of their site visits are code compliant”.

It also noted in an email the “significance of the CFMEU relationship with the Andrews government in Victoria, and the impact this is having, and will have in the future, in respect of client relationships with the Victorian government”.

Such was the extent of that relationship that John Holland officials suspected the CFMEU would have a “substantial amount of knowledge around proposed government tenders and input” in respect of the tender process.

“We are thus particularly mindful of the optics in our interactions with union stakeholders,” the 2017 email said.

Four years later, the construction company noted that “the CFMEU are emboldened by their success on major infrastructure projects,” and later in 2021, that they “enjoy a significant level of influence within the state government”.

“It is a level of influence not seen in previous years and is unlikely to diminish.”

Young told the hearing that four of the CFMEU officials who would later force work to stop at Gap Road had similarly interfered in the Metro Tunnel project.

He said the officials entered the Gap Road project’s eastern portal 29 times in January 2020, and were involved in activity considered to be unlawful, but nonetheless received attention from the government, which asked if the safety issues they raised were valid.

Young said that a “very high risk rating” was applied to the possibility of industrial action on Gap Road.

But he said that it was not until May 2022 where this risk escalated, and CFMEU officials more regularly appeared on site.

By early June, the health and safety manager for the Rail Network Alliance involved in the project, Neil Thompson, was calling police, desperate for their help in removing union officials who were stopping work including by sitting on barriers and refusing to move.

Young tendered internal notes taken on the union activity that showed there were references to the CFMEU officials warning workers that “you guys better not turn up tonight otherwise we will smash you during the day,” and that “their life would be [made] very difficult for them personally” if they continued working on the project.

A recording of a phone call between Thompson and WorkSafe and a subsequent email showed that on 6 June – the day before a meeting that is central to the commission’s hearings – he was unclear about how to get work to continue on site.

Thompson stressed the need for the union to be removed, given trains had been stopped on the line in order for a bridge to be built.

“We actually called Victoria police to escort themself off site,” Thompson said in the call to WorkSafe.

“Victoria police said there was nothing they could do. We’re going around in circles a bit because last week WorkSafe said there was nothing they could do.

“We’re just having to put up with no work happening at the moment.”

The following day, Young said, there was a 13-minute meeting, followed by a subsequent meeting at the CFMEU, which resolved the issues.

Two CFMEU officials, Joe Myles and Paul Tzimas, attended the meeting along with four officials from the project, Young told the hearing.

Young said that while the commission had obtained significant documentation regarding the events leading up to the meeting, little was known about what occurred during it and why it was significant, so the commission would be reliant on the memory witnesses had of conversations four years ago.

The key questions included what stopped the work, and whether those disruptions were used as leverage by the CFMEU, he said.

It remained unclear exactly how much the CFMEU disruption to works on the site had cost, nor what the cost was to end the disruption, Young said.

But the union interrupted work on the site for 36 hours – and traffic controllers employed after the disruption charged significantly more than those previously employed, documents in evidence before the commission show.

The hourly rate increased from about $45 to $88, or from $59 to $147 for penalty rates, the documents show.

This meant, for example, that one 10-and-a-half hour shift cost $609 under the previous firm, but $1225 for the same shift under the new firm, Young said.

The Major Transport Infrastructure Authority director general started considering the cost implications of a CFMEU campaign to increase the rates of traffic controllers about the same time the disruptions occurred on the Gap Road project.

A September briefing prepared for the then minister for transport infrastructure, Jacinta Allan, estimated that the changes could cost the authority $60m to $78m for current projects, and well in excess of $400m for those projects in planning.

Earlier on Monday, the premier, Ben Carroll, said the royal commission was one “that Victoria needed and Victorians deserve”.

“I want every Victorian to get the answers they deserve,” Carroll said.

“This is a royal commission for Victoria. It’s a tough royal commission. It’s truly independent – [it has] the broadest terms of reference possible, a special prosecutor established as well. I said I’d do this in my first press conference and now it is up and running as it should be.”

Carroll confirmed legal representation for two former premiers, Jacinta Allan and Daniel Andrews, was “supported by the state” as has been the case at “other previous royal commissions”.

The opposition leader, Jess Wilson, said: “They’ve trashed the future of this state, and now Ben Carroll is allowing the taxpayer to foot the bill for them to be defended in the royal commission, it doesn’t pass the pub test.”

#102. Russian strikes wound nine and damage Ukraine's power network ahead of talks

Employees inspect the damage of a kindergarten in Kyiv following a Russian missile attack

Overnight drone and ballistic missile attacks damaged Ukrainian energy infrastructure, President Volodymyr Zelenskyhas said, causing blackouts and disrupting water supplies.

Zelensky said 12 Ukrainian regions came under attack with the capital Kyiv hit hardest.

Nine people are reported to have been hurt across the country, including one child, in the wave of attacks that consisted of more than 30 ballistic missiles and over 170 drones.

The attacks come before Ukrainian and Russian delegations are due to take part in trilateral talks in Abu Dhabi next week.

Zelensky wrote on Telegram, external that Moscow was preparing for the talks by launching attacks on Ukraine's "critical infrastructure".

Temperatures are dropping in Ukraine, and parts of the country have had the first winter snow.

"The situation in the capital is difficult, with disruptions to electricity, water, and heating supplies. Residential buildings, the North Bridge, and a kindergarten have been damaged," Zelensky said.

"In the Kyiv region, there were strikes on substations that connect the Rivne and Khmelnytsky nuclear power plants to Kyiv and central Ukraine."

In his latest update, Kyiv's Mayor Vitali Klitschko said the city's water supply system is back to operating normally. He also said around 60% of the city's apartment buildings were connected to the heating system.

The energy minister, Denys Shmyhal, said there were power cuts in five regions but did not say how many homes were affected.

Debris and a broken window inside the kindergarten in Kyiv

Zelensky added that beyond Kyiv, substations were damaged in Vinnytsia, Zhytomyr, Rivne, Kharkiv and Dnipro regions.

The latest attacks come after US First Lady Melania Trump hosted Russian and Ukrainian delegations at the White House on Thursday.

She called for a "more enduring" ceasefire in Ukraine following a truce last week in the southern region of Kherson.

She thanked both Zelensky and Russian President Vladimir Putin, making no reference to the origins of the war, which began when Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, which has caused thousands of civilian casualties and the displacement of millions.

Hundreds of residents of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky facing starvation and constant bombardment were able to evacuate during a six-day ceasefire, which both Moscow and Kyiv credited Trump with brokering.

"The lives of innocent people must transcend the division of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace," she said.

Earlier this week, a number of people were killed when glide bombs hit an aid centre near Zaporizhzhia, with the death toll rising to 55 as teams search deeper into the ruins.

Zelensky added in his Telegram post on Saturday following the latest attacks that Ukraine was counting on increased bilateral support from the United States.

He wrote: "We are counting on unity between both parties on issues of security, support for Ukraine, condemnation of Russian aggression, and increased sanctions pressure on Putin.

"The resolve and consistency of the United States in supporting Ukraine are of great importance in bringing peace closer."

Meanwhile, Zelenksy's spokesperson would not be drawn on reports that the Ukrainian president is to be invited to the UK for a state visit next year.

#101. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#97. Italian foreign minister says hackers leaked his personal data

Antonio Tajani says the hackers also targeted his family

Italy's Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani says he has been targeted by "cyber-criminals" who published his personal data online because of his country's support for Ukraine.

"For days now, cyber-criminals have been publishing my personal data on the dark web and seeking further information, effectively exposing me to new threats and intimidation," Tajani wrote on X.

He did not specify the nature of the threats, but said the hackers were also targeting his family.

The dark web is a hidden part of the internet that is not indexed by common search engines and can only be accessed with specialist software.

"Our position on Ukraine is the political reason I have been targeted," Tajani said.

"Defending the freedom of a sovereign state, taking a clear stand in support of a nation's democracy and independence, is dangerous in the eyes of these criminal entities."

Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni blamed the incident on pro-Russian hackers and offered Tajani her "full solidarity".

"The sale of confidential information about institutional representatives is a cause of serious concern and must be tackled with the utmost determination," she said in a statement.

Moscow has not publicly responded to the allegations.

Meloni also expressed concern about "circulation of false images" which she said were shared by members of Italy's opposition. She did not give details about the content of the images.

The Italian government has provided humanitarian and military support to Ukraine in its war against Russia.

Tajani, who is also the deputy prime minister, said the government was taking the threats seriously.

#95. Paraguay's former president charged after bank collapse

Late night political drama as PeÃ±a is impeached and arrested

Prosecutors in Paraguay have charged former president Santiago PeÃ±a over his alleged role in a banking scandal that led to his impeachment.

He has been accused of breach of trust and administration for personal gain.

PeÃ±a, who denies any wrongdoing, was forced from office after a bank collapse triggered a crisis that left more than half of the country's population without access to their accounts.

He was arrested on Friday minutes after lawmakers, including from his own party, unanimously voted to remove him from office following the collapse of Ueno Bank, which has links to his private business interests.

He had offered to step down but rather than accept his resignation, senators impeached PeÃ±a and charged him with misconduct, meaning he has been stripped of the immunity usually granted to ex-presidents.

PeÃ±a, who until last year held shares in the company that owned the bank, has denied any wrongdoing.

The impeachment charges that led to PeÃ±a's removal alleged he had conflicts of interest, manipulated regulatory oversight, diverted pension funds, and participated in an institutional cover-up involving the central bank.

PeÃ±a's deputy, Pedro Alliana, has been sworn in as the new interim president and promised not to "abandon" the millions of people affected by the scandal.

"Compatriots who are now going through a difficult time as a result of the financial collapse will be fully compensated," said Alliana, who will serve as president until 2028.

The demise of the digital bank left more than half of Paraguay's population without access to their savings after it was seized by Paraguay's central bank.

Long lines of people trying to withdraw money from Ueno branches snaked down the streets of the capital Asuncion on Tuesday and Wednesday.

An estimated three million depositors remain unable to access their savings and pensions, prompting public outrage particularly in the capital, AsunciÃ³n.

Protests erupted across the country in response, with thousands taking to the streets on Thursday to demand their money.

Earlier demonstrations featured clashes with the police, who used tear gas and water cannons on the crowd.

PeÃ±a's impeachment passed with 44 out of 45 senators voting in favour of it. One lawmaker was absent.

"It was never my intention to betray the trust you placed in me," PeÃ±a said in a video posted on X announcing his resignation, adding his priority was "the peace of mind of Paraguayans who entrusted their savings to our financial system".

Afterwards, senate president Basilio NÃºÃ±ez said PeÃ±a was found guilty of "dereliction of duty".

Pedro Alliana takes the oath of office as interim President

Senator Rafael Filizzola said: "We are facing the biggest corruption scandal in our democratic history â a tremendous blow to the state, to private citizens, and to the country's credibility and image."

Analysts say PeÃ±a is likely to now be prosecuted over the bank's collapse.

His defence lawyer Ricardo Preda described the allegations as a "reckless accusation" that was "irresponsible and opportunistic".

#91. Victorian election 2026: Concern and scepticism among Labor MPs as new poll shows party trailing One Nation

Ben Carroll refuses to be drawn on latest opinion poll that puts Labor at all-time low of just 18%, behind One Nation on 21% and the Coalition on 33%

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Victorian Labor MPs are both sceptical and seriously concerned about a new poll ahead of the state election showing the party trailing third behind One Nation, with its primary vote at an all-time low of just 18%.

The DemosAU/PremierNational, first published by the Herald Sun on Monday, showed Labor’s primary vote share has crashed seven points to 18%, putting it in third place behind the Coalition’s 33% and One Nation’s 21%. At the same time, the Greens improved their primary vote to 17%, an increase of three points since September.

It means the Coalition now leads Labor on a two-party preferred basis 56-44, compared with 52-48 in September.

George Hasanakos, DemosAU’s head of research, said if the poll were replicated on election night then many contests across the state would be between the Liberals or Nationals and One Nation, which would then result in a “clear Coalition majority”.

“If that happens, One Nation has to poll really, really well to have a chance of winning, because Labor and Greens preferences will flow to the Liberals,” he said.

On election night, the Victorian Electoral Commission plans to conduct a two-candidate-preferred count, or 2CP, between the two candidates it expects to finish with the most support after preferences are distributed. The commission is selecting the indicative 2CP for each seat based on polling, analysis of preference flows and results from previous elections. It will not publish the indicative 2CPs before election day to avoid influencing voter behaviour.

Hasanakos added that he “double, triple, quadruple checked” the result of the “extraordinary” poll, which had 1,562 respondents, an effective sample of 818 and a margin of error of 3.4%.

“We went to the trouble of a bigger sample here, and I stand by it,” he said.

Ben Carroll repeatedly refused to say whether he was worried about the poll at a press conference in South Melbourne on Monday morning.

“What I’m focused on every day is fighting for working people and making sure that every Victorian that goes into the ballot on November 28 knows that under my government you have a government on your side that will fight for you,” he told reporters.

“The alternative is a Liberal-One Nation government that will fight against you – they’re going to shrink the economy, cause a recession and make life difficult for every single Victorian.”

But the result has sparked serious concerns among the Labor caucus, even though some MPs are sceptical about the 18% figure. Inside Labor’s campaign headquarters, they are treating the result as an “outlier” and point to other public polls that have their primary vote in the 20s.

Several Labor MPs, unauthorised to speak publicly, have told Guardian Australia there are many in the caucus questioning their party’s strategy.

This includes a focus in recent days on crime – an issue that is typically a Coalition strength. On Sunday, Carroll announced a $204m plan to enable judges to sentence low- and mid-level youth offenders to a new rehabilitation detention program, while on Monday he announced $70m to recruit 200 more police reservists to reopen stations and build and upgrade more stations.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“Caucus is really angry and deflated, because this is all happening because of him,” one MP said.

“Even if it’s higher than 18, and we get back into something with a two in front of it, the experiment has been an epic fail, the campaign is in freefall, and it’s about to get a whole lot worse.”

Another MP said the focus on crime was “diabolically stupid strategy” as the government had years to tackle community concern around rising rates.

The opposition leader, Jess Wilson, on Monday said her party had a focus on “getting tough on crime”, including by introducing Aidan’s Law to toughen sentencing for attacks on people who step in to protect others.

Carroll immediately matched the commitment to introduce the proposed law, named after 22-year-old Aidan Becker, who was fatally stabbed at Mernda station after stepping in to protect a 14-year-old during an alleged violent robbery.

It comes as Pauline Hanson rejected reports by the Age and 60 Minutes on Sunday that her former national campaign director Sean Black continues to secretly work for One Nation in Victoria.

Black in 2018 was jailed for rape and subsequently lost his appeal against the conviction but was then rehired by One Nation in 2020 as a campaign director after serving his time. Hanson sacked him after political pressure earlier this year.

She told the Australian Financial Review on Monday “the day that I sacked him [Sean Black] that was the day he finished up”.

#90. Iran executes three men for 'armed unrest' after January protests

Reza Zanganeh was one of the three men executed in Iran

Iran has executed three men accused of carrying out armed unrest during January's anti-government protests, according to state media.

Hassan Dioband, Hamid Kazemi and Reza Zanganeh were accused of throwing Molotov cocktails and grenades, using bladed weapons and firearms, and setting fire to public facilities in the city of Karaj, west of Tehran, Iran's state news agency Tasnim, external reported.

The executions are the latest in a series linked to the protests, including those of people accused of having ties to Israel and the United States.

The number of executions in Iran has increased since US-Israeli strikes on the Islamic Republic began in late February.

The protest movement initially sparked in December last year by the high cost of living rapidly evolved into widespread anti-government demonstrations that peaked on 8 and 9 of January.

Iranian authorities reported more than 3,000 deaths, and blamed the violence on "terrorist acts" orchestrated by the US and Israel.

However foreign-based NGOs gave higher figures and the BBC received multiple accounts from Iranians of security forces opening fire on unarmed protesters.

Tasnim reported that the three men were executed after the Supreme Court upheld their convictions and found them guilty of "participating in operational activities on behalf of the Israeli Zionist regime, the United States and hostile groups".

The damage allegedly included an electricity substation in Karaj which caused power outages in some areas.

Dioband was arrested on 11 January after investigations identified him as a suspect in attacks on public property and incitement to violence.

Ten others accused of taking part in the unrest under his supervision were later arrested.

Reuters news agency reports that they received prison terms of 5 to 10 years.

The latest executions in Iran come after the UN human rights chief Volker TÃ¼rk said he was alarmed by the rise in death penalties.

In August, TÃ¼rk said at least 56 people had been executed on national security-related charges since 19 March, including 27 in cases linked to January's nationwide anti-government protests.

Iran is thought to be the world's second-most prolific executioner after China. Last year, it put to death at least 2,159 people, according to Amnesty International.

The human rights group said that was the highest figure on record since 1981 and included 45 executions of suspected dissidents on national security-related charges.

#88. Stop undermining Jim Chalmers, environment minister urges Labor colleagues

‘It’s pretty obvious that there are all sorts of people making all sorts of comments,’ Murray Watt says after budget savings meeting

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The environment minister, Murray Watt, has urged ministers to stop undermining the treasurer, Jim Chalmers, as Labor struggles to sell its budget and looks to cut popular clean energy subsidies to help with the fight against inflation.

Cabinet ministers met again on Monday to discuss potential budget savings to be included in next month’s mid-year update, part of efforts to reducing spending to ease pressure on the economy.

Watt was asked about speculation of tension between the treasurer and the prime minister, Anthony Albanese, and whether he’d like to see an end to “undermining and leaking” against Chalmers.

“Yes, I would,” Watt said. “As a member of the government, I take seriously the responsibility to work together to support government policies and also to get our work to support each other in what are very difficult times.

“I think that Jim Chalmers is doing an excellent job as a treasurer in what are very difficult economic times; many of the features that are impacting on our economy are sort of well outside any treasurer’s control, and I very much support Jim Chalmers in his efforts.”

Watt conceded Chalmers was being undermined from within Labor.

“It’s pretty obvious that there are all sorts of people making all sorts of comments.”

A new round of opinion polls added to Labor’s political pain on Monday, with the Resolve Political Monitor in the Age and Sydney Morning Herald showing Labor’s primary vote falling to 28 percentage points, down from 34.6% at last year’s election.

The Coalition also dropped one point to 22%, while One Nation was steady at 25%.

A Redbridge poll in the Financial Review showed Labor’s primary vote slipped two percentage points to 26%, while the Coalition’s jumped four points to 25%. One Nation was down one point to 28%.

Monday’s meeting of the expenditure review committee of cabinet comes after last week’s interest rate hike from the Reserve Bank. The governor, Michele Bullock, said while it is up to governments to manage their budgets independently of the RBA, public and private spending had caused “excess demand” in the economy.

Watt accused the Coalition and minor parties of leaving existing spending proposals to linger in parliament, including savings from changes to the private health insurance rebate for over 65s.

“Let’s not forget that for all of their demands that we find more savings, the Coalition currently are blocking the government from saving $10 billion in measures that are before the parliament,” Watt said.

Albanese has confirmed spending in the public service will be scrutinised as the government looks for savings in the budget update, expected in the middle of December. Last year, departments and agencies were asked to identify potential savings of as much as 5% from their annual budgets, part of the government’s efforts to cut lower-priority spending.

Spending on Labor’s $7bn home battery subsidy program and incentives for electric vehicle purchases could be reined in.

Announced in April 2025, the batteries program was originally designed to cost about $2bn. More than 600,000 systems have been installed, but the program could have means testing rules added.

Separately, subsidies designed to promote uptake of electric vehicles has cost the budget far more than originally anticipated.

Angus Taylor announced an opposition “red tape razor gang” on Monday, designed to reduce the burden on business from costly regulation.

#86. 'Flat', 'tired', 'cynical': Unusually gloomy Kiwis deliver New Zealand's tightest election in years

New Zealand election 'too close to call' - so who's won?

New Zealand voted in a new government as fog choked the capital, Wellington, on Saturday - and the mood was as palpable as the grey weather.

"It's pretty flat," 21-year-old Imogen Vlutger says. "There's this kind of apathy and a bit of dread."

"Tired. I think we're all tired," Symone, 28, admits.

"I didn't even vote," one man in his 40s sheepishly confesses. He couldn't bring himself to tick any of the boxes, he explains, declining to chat further.

"Wellington's a bit of a bubble," another voter says, expressing a caveat I heard often, before describing feeling disillusioned.

But polling shows this is a pervasive sentiment across the country: the national mood is low and faith in politics is even lower.

More than half the country no longer sees it as a land of opportunity, a recent study found, while a similar portion fears that the future looks even worse.

Many Kiwis feel the economy is in ruins, there are no jobs, the cost of living has become sky high, the sense of community that used to be a source of national pride is breaking down and there's no sense of genuine progress on policy solutions.

A record number of them are abandoning ship in search of brighter futures elsewhere.

Saturday's election also saw a record number of voters desert the two major parties, giving neither a clear mandate.

But it brought new highs for parties across the spectrum: the left-leaning Greens, the right-wing libertarian Act Party and the centrist Opportunity Party â the latter of which had never won a seat in parliament before but could now hold the balance of power with seven.

The Opportunity Party says it is willing to work with either of the major parties

"You have voted to change New Zealand politics for good," the party's ecstatic leader Quilae Wong told Kiwis on election night. "I personally came into this because I was frustrated at the slow pace of change."

It will likely be weeks before we know exactly who will make up the governing coalition and what deals have been made to form it.

But this is only the first of many challenges facing whoever is the next prime minister of a country at a crossroads.

New Zealand hasn't always felt like this.

For a long time, the country was viewed as a land of milk and honey â literally â for those who worked hard, with its people ranking amongst the most positive in the world.

A satisfying career, access to quality healthcare, and a close network of family and friends â which research suggests are the three most important building blocks of a happy life â were all there for the taking, University of Auckland Prof Robert MacCulloch says.

"The lifestyle was good," the world-leader in the study of happiness economics tells the BBC. "You weren't the highest paid, but you could get by nicely."

Many young people like Michael Dawson (left) feel like they can't get ahead

Many who spoke to the BBC said the country's decline accelerated into a dive during the Covid pandemic.

Unemployment is now at an 11-year high and rising. For Kiwis aged under 24, things haven't been this bad in three decades: jobs are so scarce that one in three either cannot get enough work or have none at all.

Michael Dawson says he did everything he thought he was supposed to: finished school, studied a trade and obtained a qualification, and has applied for dozens of jobs.

But it's been a year now and the job market is so dire he hasn't had a single interview.

"It has sucked. A lot... I'm somewhat cynical with the way I think [about the future] now," the 20-year-old says.

Zainab Ali, chief executive of support service Youth Inspire, has never seen demand so high, with waitlists for its employment aid workshops stretching into the hundreds.

The young people who do make it through their doors on the outskirts of Wellington are disheartened.

"The things we used to hear from everyone's grumpy uncle around the Christmas table sometimes, our young people are now starting to give us that same feedback: like, 'What's the point?'"

Sam says he's flirted with a move to Australia

Meanwhile, household electricity bills have jumped drastically, partly driven by global supply shocks. New Zealand remains one of the least affordable places on Earth to rent or buy a home. High inflation means every dollar is having to stretch further.

The island nation produces enough food to feed its population eight times over, but about a quarter of adults are skipping meals because the price of groceries â set by a supermarket duopoly â is among the highest in the developed world.

And the headlines are full of healthcare crises: long waiting lists, chronic understaffing, crumbling health infrastructure and climbing costs for patients. Few stories have captured the state of the system like the death of a man who collapsed in an emergency department toilet after a nine-hour wait in July.

"There's a segment of the population that are facing real material hardship... and then there's another segment of New Zealanders that are quite anxious that they could be next," political scientist Luke Oldfield, from Victoria University of Wellington, says.

Increasingly polarised public debate â around topics like MÄori rights and the Treaty of Waitangi, healthcare for LGBT+ people and immigration â have added to the mental load for many in those communities.

"There's been a few groups feeling like they're being used as a political football," Dr Lara Greaves, who studies Indigenous politics, says.

This perfect storm is driving about 180 people to leave the country every day â a record-breaking exodus that is dividing families, shrinking communities and draining talent pools.

Siblings Sam and Emily tell the BBC that they love New Zealand but find it hard to keep finding reasons to stay. Their friends are leaving en masse and even their parents have considered moving to Australia.

"It's not just that you're being rewarded for going to Australia, it's that you're kind of actively being punished for staying here," Sam says.

Stu thinks New Zealand is on its way back up

There are still large parts of the country that feel differently, though.

At the nation's largest livestock saleyard in the town of Feilding, where farmers from all over the island have gathered, Stu Morton says the last year was the best he's had in his 30-year career.

The agriculture industry - which makes up about 3% of New Zealand's GDP - was struggling under the previous Labour government's emissions reductions scheme, he adds, but has turned a corner under the National Party.

He believes the rest of the economy will follow suit.

"We've been through those times where you're questioning 'Where's the future?'

"What helps is having a stable government... [and] it comes down to a positive attitude - appreciating that what you've got is actually okay."

Many of these issues affecting New Zealand are global trends - and it's undeniable that Kiwis' standard of living is better than many others.

But these issues are having a corrosive impact on its national identity.

"It leads to a crisis of confidence about what New Zealand is and where it's going," Helen Clark, who was prime minister from 1999 to 2008, tells the BBC.

Her foundation surveys New Zealanders every year about social cohesion, and its most recent report found Kiwis - in particular young people - feel far more isolated than they did a generation ago, attitudes towards multiculturalism are souring and the population is becoming less hopeful.

Faith in institutions and leaders has taken an alarming turn: only two out of five New Zealanders trust the government to do the right thing, while less than half of the country trusts the courts.

"There's a feeling that, really, there's no-one who cares," Clark says.

National Party leader Christopher Luxon is seeking a second term

The transformation former Labour prime minister Jacinda Ardern promised on issues like healthcare and housing when she was elected in 2017 never eventuated. Many other achievements - for example world leading smoking reforms, emissions reduction policies or MÄori language initiatives - have been wound back by her successors.

Her legacy is dominated by polarising Covid decisions, as is Chris Hipkins', who was health minister before his eight months in the top job after Ardern's resignation.

The National Party came to power in 2023 promising to "get our country back on track" and rebuild the economy.

"You'd have to say they've had very limited success," political commentator Grant Duncan says. "And the Luxon government can only blame the previous government for so long."

National delivered the education overhaul it pledged, but the biggest moment of their tenure was the massive hikoi â protest march â led by MÄori over their coalition partner's unpopular bill to redefine the Treaty of Waitangi. And then came the two leadership challenges Christopher Luxon fought off in the past six months.

Former PM Chris Hipkins has been leader of New Zealand's centre-left Labour Party since January 2023

There's a sense Labour had too much ambition, much of it costly and unfulfilled, while National doesn't have enough, having spent much of its time and effort wrangling their own flank.

Both governments say their plans for the country were stymied by crises out of their control â such as Covid, the Iran war and US trade antagonism.

But Duncan says that's not cutting it with voters. He's never seen the country quite like this before: "Neither of the parties has been able to field a really strong, inspiring leader. Both of them have a track record of disappointing results."

No matter who ends up taking the top job, Sam says he's not convinced much will change.

"It does just feel stale... a lot of the population probably feels like we could do a bit better," he says. With a wry smile, he adds: "I don't think it helps that they're two white guys named Chris."

#85. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”
