#84. There’s more than a whiff of politics to the sudden call to slash Labor’s green energy subsidies

EVs and home batteries are in Treasury crosshairs despite a better economic case for scaling back nuclear-powered submarines and diesel fuel rebates for miners

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

As Jim Chalmers, the treasurer, comes under increasing pressure to fight inflation by slashing spending in the mid-year budget update, the drumbeat is loudest for cuts in one specific area: green energy subsidies for households.

Receiving the most focus is the cheaper home batteries program, which from July last year has offered a taxpayer-backed 30% rebate to households and businesses.

The scheme has become a victim of its own success. Treasury officials originally estimated it would lead to a million new batteries by 2030 at a cost of $2.3bn. The latest estimate is more than triple that figure, and only after a tweak to the policy.

Also in the crosshairs is the 2022 electric vehicle subsidy, which exempts workers from fringe benefits tax when they lease an EV through their employer and has proved more popular – and expensive – than predicted.

Despite reports that Treasury is pushing for cuts to the home battery scheme, the argument has hardly been made.

There is more than a whiff of politics to the sudden and urgent need to do away with clean energy programs, against, say, scaling back billions of dollars in diesel fuel rebates for miners.

When budget experts are asked broader questions about the big pressures on the federal government’s finances, rarely do they cite handouts for home batteries or tax breaks for electric vehicles.

In a speech on Monday, Angus Taylor, the opposition leader, again promised to “abolish Labor’s ideological climate bureaucracy” as part of his promised $31bn of budget savings over four years.

Meanwhile, the Albanese government’s recent efforts to apply an income test to private healthcare rebates for over-65s was met with howls of protest from the Coalition.

As Stephen Bartos, an economics professor at the University of Canberra, says, in all this talk about cutting spending, “nobody is mentioning nuclear-powered submarines”.

Bartos, a former deputy secretary at the Department of Finance and New South Wales parliamentary budget officer, says if the challenge is to close the budget deficit, then green subsidies “shouldn’t be their first port of call”.

“Partly because it’s small beer compared to the big-ticket items like health, NDIS and defence. But also because there is a rationale for green subsidies, if a declining one.”

The national disability insurance scheme, for example, was expected to cost nearly $54bn in 2025–26, which has made reining in the cost of the scheme an existential question for the government’s fiscal plans. In 2023, the Parliamentary Budget Office estimated the total cost of acquiring nuclear-powered submarines under the Aukus agreement to be as much $368bn over the life of the program.

The federal fuel tax credit scheme, which mainly helps major global mining companies, costs about $11bn a year and is rising fast.

Bartos is critical of government tax breaks for electric vehicles, but says the case for public support for batteries is stronger.

“I would probably be looking at where the price signals encouraging adoption [of clean energy] are weakest, and that’s probably batteries. For EVs, people are shifting no matter what.”

The case for the cheaper batteries scheme has also been undermined by Treasury officials’ abject failure to predict how popular it would be. A month into the program and it was already obvious they had got it wrong, as the expected cost blew out towards $14bn.

Not only was our appetite for subsidised batteries way higher than official forecasts, the original design of the scheme made it more desirable to buy bigger, more expensive batteries.

There has since been a tweak to the design to account for that second issue, which is how Treasury now predicts – for what it’s worth – that the program will cost $7.2bn.

These cost blowouts have amplified derision of the home batteries as “middle-class welfare” of the worst sort.

Alison Reeve, the director of the energy and climate change program at the Grattan Institute, understands the concerns, but says the evidence to back that claim “is pretty thin when you look at where the batteries are being installed”.

Notably, this is in homes across outer suburbia, such as western Sydney, and in regional areas. In other words, not in enclaves of the comfortably well-off.

Then there is the bigger-picture issue: that we risk sacrificing long-term clean energy goals in the name of short-term fiscal objectives.

“Batteries are going to be a big part of our energy future,” Reeve says. “We do need to do some stimulating of that industry until it can stand on its own two feet.”

Slashing support for batteries would damage the burgeoning web of small businesses, suppliers and tradies who have invested in servicing the infant batteries industry.

That’s not to say there are ways to improve the cost and benefits trade-off in the home batteries program. This could be by ensuring subsidised batteries deliver a community benefit by contributing to the power grid in times of need.

“We need to make sure we are maximising the public benefit, rather than just the private benefit,” Reeve says.

There are lessons in how Western Australia has designed its home batteries support scheme.

“If you look at the west coast, you couldn’t get the state subsidy unless you linked yourself up to a virtual power plan run by the network operator. That was in order to benefit the whole network,” she says.

“We also need a clear path out of the subsidy so it doesn’t grow uncontrollably.”

#81. ‘Exceedingly heavy-handed’: ACT police criticised by magistrate for Tasering disabled Aboriginal woman

Exclusive: Body-worn camera footage shows woman pulled off bench and a Taser activated despite ‘putting up no resistance’, magistrate says

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A magistrate has criticised “exceedingly heavy-handed” ACT police officers who Tasered an Aboriginal woman with an intellectual disability during a routine arrest.

The woman, named Emma, pleaded with officers not to use the stun gun on her and told them she was unarmed during the arrest at a Canberra bus stop in January.

Police were called because of reports Emma had a knife, but she was only found to have two pairs of scissors, one of which was broken, in a nearby backpack.

The court was shown police body-worn footage of the arrest, in which she is seen raising her hands immediately after officers arrived and pointed a Taser towards her.

As she pushed back against an officer who was trying to handcuff her she was pulled to the ground.

While two officers held her down another placed a Taser against her back and activated it, in what is known as “drive stun” mode. Seven officers were present at the time.

The ACT magistrates court heard in September that officers also threatened to use OC spray against Emma after she appeared to self-harm by deliberately striking her head against a police van during the arrest.

A transcript of the hearing was released by the court to Guardian Australia in October.

Special magistrate Sean Richter, a former South Australian police officer, said he found the conduct of officers “incredibly” and “exceedingly” heavy-handed, and agreed that they were aware of who Emma was and her vulnerabilities before they arrested her.

“I sometimes wonder when we lost the ability to speak to people,” Richter said.

“She’s a woman that was putting up no resistance.

“She’s sitting on the bench with her arms in the air.”

Richter said Emma was involved in a motor vehicle accident when she was 12 and received a traumatic brain injury.

He said the ACT civil and administrative tribunal described her as having a mental impairment and a neurocognitive disorder, and said she had been placed on a psychiatric treatment order.

Emma was charged with multiple offences by police in relation to the incident, but all of them except possessing a knife without reasonable excuse were withdrawn by the prosecution. She pleaded guilty to that offence, which related to the scissors in her bag, and was convicted. Emma also faced Richter on other matters unrelated to the arrest.

“I was shown the video of her arrest on the charge of possess knife without reasonable excuse. I will say this; I consider that the actions of the police in that matter were unwarranted and heavy-handed,” Richter said.

“Here was a woman sitting on a park bench putting her arms in the air. She’s clearly known to the police because one of them names her.

“It’s blatantly apparent to anyone that deals with this woman that she has a significant cognitive disorder and should be treated as such. I find it amazing that in this day and age the police reacted in the way that they did.”

In a statement, ACT policing said it “notes the comments of Special Magistrate Richter in relation to the arrest of a woman in January 2026.

“As an associated civil claim is under way in relation to this matter, it would not be appropriate to provide further comment.”

In that civil claim filed in July, Emma’s lawyer, Michael Lalor, accused one of the officers involved in the arrest of misfeasance in public office.

The officer is accused of making a series of misleading statements or omissions in police evidence that resulted in more serious charges being laid, and Emma spending 28 days in custody before bail was granted after her lawyers viewed the body-worn camera footage of the arrest.

The statement of claim alleges the misleading statements include that Emma ignored police commands, that she had “abruptly” bent down towards her bag, that she attempted to strike an officer multiple times in the face, and that she was Tasered because she engaged in a “high level of violence”.

Lalor also writes in the civil claim that it would be alleged police provided misleading statements regarding the witness who called officers saying they had seen a woman with a knife.

Police said that the civilian witness had “immediately feared for his safety and departed swiftly” after seeing Emma, but the statement of claim said police failed to make clear the man and his partner “were inside a moving vehicle with the doors locked at the relevant time”.

That witness also told police, according to the statement of claim, that “I wasn’t particularly scared, I was in a car with the doors locked, so I knew she couldn’t really stab me.”

Lalor is also alleging breaches of the Human Rights Act and exemplary damages for assault and battery.

Police have not yet been required to file a defence to the claim.

ACT police have come under repeated recent scrutiny for their use of force, particularly against Aboriginal people.

In August, the commonwealth and ACT ombudsman criticised police for failing to clearly define “racial profiling” after officers in Canberra pulled guns on an Indigenous teenager they mistook for a suspect in an aggravated burglary.

In June last year, the ACT ombudsman found that in about a third of cases it reviewed, unprofessional behaviour by officers such as impatience, aggression, foul and abusive language appeared to unnecessarily escalate situations.

This led to “use of force in circumstances where a more sensitive and trauma-informed approach to negotiation and de-escalation might have avoided the need to use force or as much force”.

As part of that investigation, the ombudsman recommended that police identify and implement measures to better equip its officers with a greater understanding of “mental illness and how it affects a person’s behaviour and ability to comply with directions”, but police did not accept the recommendation.

#80. Aramco Tower hit in heart of Saudi capital as Houthis launch fresh strikes

The Aramco Tower, a skyscraper in the Saudi Arabian capital's central financial district, had been hit by an air attack.

A statement issued via the Houthi-controlled Saba news agency confirmed the group had targeted Riyadh's business zone on Saturday, without mentioning the Aramco Tower by name.

The Saudi energy ministry said the tower had suffered only "minor damage" and no one had been killed or injured.

Footage authenticated by BBC Verify shows more extensive damage to the building, with at least one floor appearing to have been badly hit by a missile or drone strike.

Windows on more than one level appear to have been shattered by the strike.

The Houthis also said Riyadh's airport and Aramco oil storage facilities were targeted in separate strikes. Saudi Arabia said its air defences had intercepted and destroyed several missiles.

The strikes were the latest claimed by the Yemen-based armed group against targets deep inside Saudi Arabia, which the Gulf power has struggled to contain in recent months.

It came a day after the Houthis accused a Saudi-led coalition of killing at least 33 people, including women and children, in an air strike on Yemen.

Footage broadcast by Houthi-run television channel Al Masirah showed rescuers pulling bodies from the rubble of several buildings in al-Shariyah, a town to the east of the capital, Sanaa.

Saba said 39 people had been injured in the strike. Saudi Arabia has not commented.

A Saudi-led coalition has been fighting the Houthis for more than a decade in support of Yemen's internationally recognised government, which has lost control of a large swathe of the country to the rebel group.

Although the conflict had largely been dormant in recent years, fighting has intensified following the outbreak of the Iran war.

An informal ceasefire unravelled in July after the Tehran-backed Houthis announced a "maritime embargo" against Saudi Arabia and began missile and drone attacks on Saudi airports, oil facilities and tankers in the Red Sea.

The Houthis said they were retaliating for what they have called a Saudi blockade of their territory, as well as an air strike on Sanaa airport Saudi Arabia said had been to prevent an Iranian aircraft from landing there.

Major fighting has since resumed between Houthi forces and troops aligned with Yemen's Saudi-backed government.

The United Nations says more than 1,600 people have been killed and more than 300,000 displaced since fighting resumed.

#79. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#75. Global stocks fall amid ‘big worries over AI’ after Democrats’ midterms triumph

Asian and European markets follow US tech rout with many analysts predicting more waves of selling to come

European and Asian stock markets fell sharply on Friday after the Democrats’ decisive victory in the midterms triggered a rout in US tech stocks a day earlier.

A sell-off on Wall Street on Thursday was followed by sharp falls in Asian stock markets on Friday. The investor sentiment then reached London, where the market was, at one stage, poised for its worst day since March.

The FTSE 100 index, which tracks blue-chip shares listed in London, was down as much as 254 points, or 2.35%, at 10,528 points during a wave of selling in morning trading. But by the close of the day it had clawed back some of its losses to be down 1%, helped by a rise in US stocks when Wall Street reopened.

Germany’s Dax index fell by 2%, France’s Cac 40 dropped by 0.8% and Spain’s Ibex lost 0.6%.

Susannah Streeter, the chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said: “The tech sell-off has intensified, with the pain of the brutal sell-off on Wall Street carried straight through to Asia and knocking sentiment in Europe.

“Big worries are colliding – the build-out of datacentres looks set to slow, which risks shredding the spending spree underpinning the boom, right at the time that investors have become super nervous about returns on this massive investment.”

However, there were signs of easing jitters on Wall Street on Friday, where tech stocks rose, lifting the S&P 500 share index by 0.3% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq closed up 1% – which only recovered a portion of their previous day’s losses.

That followed a stronger-than-expected employment report that showed the US economy added 165,000 new jobs in October, pulling the unemployment rate down to 4.1%.

On Thursday, the Nasdaq share index had its worst day in 19 months after slumping 5.5%.

Chip stocks led the slump on Thursday, with the memory maker SanDisk tumbling by 22% and the hard drive maker Western Digital falling by 17%. Wall Street’s “fear index”, the Vix, jumped by more than 60% to its highest levels since the early weeks of the Iran war, in a sign of rising market volatility.

The heavy falls continued in Asia on Friday, with Japan’s Nikkei 225 index sinking 6% and South Korea’s Kospi index tumbling more than 6.5%.

“It’s actually a bit of a disaster,” said Christopher Forbes, the head of Asia and Middle East at CMC Markets.

“[Clients] were long index, long stocks, and they all got carried out. All night, all day – we had client margin calls, client complaints … because it’s a very emotional thing, right? People are wedded to their positions and views on a stock or outcome, and you’re telling them: ‘Look, you’ve just lost money.’”

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said: “Yesterday’s [Thursday’s] brutal session marked a dramatic turnaround from Wednesday, but with AI fears still prominent there is a risk that there is much more selling to come.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

“Investors remain on watch for a potential resumption of the Iran conflict, and are nervously watching the oil price after its slump earlier in the week. All in all it remains a very febrile atmosphere.”

However, despite the sharp losses suffered on global stock markets around the world, most indices are still up for the year. The Nasdaq Composite index remains up 6.35% since the start of January – even though it is more than 10% below its peak.

The FTSE 100 has gained almost 7.5% in 2026 – but it is now more than 300 points below the record high set in July.

Mohit Kumar, an economist at the investment bank Jefferies, said that while he did not expect a quick rebound from Thursday’s losses in the US, he also did not believe it marked the “start of the unwind of the AI trade”.

He said: “AI investment has been a key driver of US growth and we do not see the Democrats as giving up on this growth engine. Datacentres and AI infrastructure also create jobs and Democrats would be reluctant to let those jobs move on to other states or outside the country.”

Stock market tremors have also hit the London Metal Exchange, with copper poised for its biggest weekly fall since March.

Benchmark three-month copper, a key metal used in the semiconductor, electronics and automotive industry, has dropped 0.61%, leaving it positioned for a 2.79% fall this week.

Craig Lang, a principal analyst at the consultancy CRU, said the price of copper was increasingly influenced by the AI market and “sentiment towards datacentre expansion”.

#74. Polluted Delhi Tries ‘Green’ Fireworks to Clear the Air at Diwali

India’s top court decreed that only more environmentally friendly fireworks could be set off this weekend, citing air quality concerns. But environmental activists call it a “green wash.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#73. Homes and temples glow across India to mark Diwali

Millions across India are celebrating Diwali, the Hindu festival of lights, illuminating homes, temples, offices and streets with oil lamps and colourful lights to symbolise the triumph of light over darkness.

The festival falls on the darkest night of the Hindu lunar month of Kartik, when the moon is not visible in the sky.

Although Diwali traditions vary across India's regions and communities, the festival is widely marked by prayers, family gatherings, the exchange of sweets and the lighting of firecrackers.

But the celebrations come amid growing concerns over air pollution, particularly in northern India.

Seasonal weather conditions and the burning of crop residue contribute to deteriorating air quality, while firecrackers set off during Diwali can make the situation worse.

Air pollution in the Indian capital Delhi has reached such hazardous levels that it is the most polluted Diwali festival in a decade. New measures to improve air quality do not seem to have been successful.

India's financial capital, Mumbai, has also experienced high pollution levels in recent weeks.

In an effort to limit pollution, India's Supreme Court on Monday permitted the manufacture, sale and use of certified "green" firecrackers in Delhi and surrounding areas for a limited period.

These firecrackers are designed to produce 20-30% fewer emissions than conventional fireworks. But critics have questioned how effective they are at reducing pollution and raised concerns that conventional firecrackers could still make their way into the capital from neighbouring states.

Repeated violations of firecracker restrictions leave cities grappling with worsening post-Diwali smog

Despite the debate over fireworks, Diwali is about much more than firecrackers.

The festival celebrates the return of Lord Ram to Ayodhya after his victory over Ravan, the main antagonist in the Hindu epic Ramayana, and symbolises the triumph of good over evil, light over darkness, and knowledge over ignorance.

In the days leading up to the festival, families clean and decorate their homes with flowers, colourful lights and traditional oil lamps known as diyas.

The celebrations also bring families and friends together for prayers, elaborate feasts and the exchange of gifts, and lots of traditional Indian sweets.

Colourful patterns known as rangoli are created at entrances to homes, while markets bustle with shoppers buying festive essentials beneath brightly lit decorations.

Potters make and paint clay lamps, or diyas, that are lit during the festival

The celebrations began on 6 November with Dhanteras, when buying gold, silver or new utensils is considered auspicious and believed to bring good fortune.

The second day, known as Chhoti Diwali, or small Diwali, is marked by the lighting of oil lamps and, in some households, setting off firecrackers.

The third day is the main celebration, when families offer evening prayers to Lakshmi, the Hindu goddess of wealth and prosperity, before gathering for festive meals and fireworks.

Offered during prayers and shared among family and friends, sweets are an integral part of the celebrations

The fourth day is celebrated differently across India, with family gatherings and New Year festivities in some communities. The fifth and final day, known as Bhai Dooj, celebrates the bond between brothers and sisters.

The festival is also celebrated by Sikhs and Jains along with other important religious observances.

Sikhs mark Bandi Chhor Divas, or Prisoner Release Day, commemorating the release of their sixth guru, Guru Hargobind, from imprisonment at Gwalior Fort in the early 17th Century.

For Jains, Diwali marks the attainment of moksha, or liberation from the cycle of rebirth, by Lord Mahavira, the 24th and last Tirthankara, or spiritual teacher, of the current era.

With Indian communities spread across the world, Diwali is also celebrated internationally, with families and friends coming together for festive gatherings and exchanging gifts.

Parts of north India are covered with lights of every colour and shade

The festival is considered an auspicious time to make new purchases, which many believe bring prosperity and good fortune. India's stock exchanges even hold a special one-hour trading session for investors who consider buying and selling shares on Diwali favourable. Known as Muhurat trading, it will take place on 8 November this year.

Although rooted in Hindu traditions, Diwali is celebrated by people across different faiths and communities.

Pets and strays are honoured in Nepal during Diwali, where the festival is known as Tihar

#70. New Zealand election coalition talks will likely take weeks – as it happened

This blog is closed, you can read our report on the New Zealand election here

Thanks for following the blog tonight. The election has ended without a clear winner. Here are the top news lines from what will go down as one of New Zealand’s closest elections in many decades.

Christopher Luxon’s National party and its right-leaning coalition partners are on the brink of a wafer-thin majority that could allow it to eke out a return to power.

The left-wing bloc, led by Labour’s Chris Hipkins, fell short of a majority.

Neither Luxon or Hipkins claimed victory or conceded defeat. Both acknowledged an expected wait before a governing coalition is negotiated.

A centrist newcomer, the Opportunity party, has entered parliament, polling above the 5% threshold. They could potentially be a kingmaker in a coalition.

Final results are expected on 27 November. Coalition talks will likely take weeks.

Hundreds of thousands of special votes are yet to be counted and could change the shape of parliament.

Saturday figures showed there could be a 130-seat parliament, with 66 seats needed for a majority.

Results suggested a coalition of National, Act and NZ First would hold 67 seats.

The 67 included 42 for National, 13 for Act and 12 for NZ First.

The left-bloc totalled 56 seats: Labour 35, Greens 16 and 5 for Te Pāti Māori.

Opportunity is projected to gain 7 seats.

Over the coming days and weeks we will get a sense of the magnitude of Qiulae Wong and the Opportunity party’s success tonight. Opportunity are currently projected to claim seven seats in parliament.

Wong has said that if she held the tiebreaker power, she was open to negotiating with either a conservative or a left-wing government and would deal first with whichever major party won the most votes.

The right-wing bloc might not need her, and Luxon has ruled out governing with Opportunity. Analysts say, however, that both major party leaders would likely answer the phone if Wong held the only path to power.

You can read more about the rise of Opportunity here, in this piece from Michelle Duff.

A couple of pics of Christopher Luxon as he arrived to speak to supporters tonight.

On the current count, the Greens are celebrating a best-ever result of what looks to be 16 seats in parliament.

With 0.56% support, Te Pāti Māori will be stoked with its result tonight, writes Jamie Tahana.

It seems odd, but the Māori party has spent the election deliberately asking people not to give them their party vote. Instead, they asked for electorate votes only.

Just a reminder, seven of the seats in New Zealand’s parliament are reserved for representatives of Māori voters – you can read more about them here.

The party’s co-leaders, Rawiri Waititi and Debbie Ngarewa-Packer, have comfortably won their respective seats of Waiariki and Te Tai Hauauru; Oriini Kaipara swept the Auckland seat of Tamaki Makaurau; Hana Rawhiti Maipi-Clarke, who hit international stardom for her haka in parliament, has cruised across the line in Hauraki-Waikato; while Aperahama Edwards holds a narrow lead over the Greens’ Hūhana Lydon in the far North seat of Te Tai Tokerau.

That’s five seats. But with a party vote so low, they will be five overhang seats.

Still, after a term that has seen plenty of in-fighting in Te Pāti Māori, there are signs of frustration in the Māori electorate, with Waititi and Ngarewa-Packer seeing significantly reduced majorities.

The Greens have fallen short of gaining their first ever Māori electorate, but across the seats party vote has risen to about 40%, making the party a significant force in Māori politics.

Labour, having been punished by the Māori electorate in 2023, has improved, particularly in terms of party vote, and has claimed a new MP in Mananui Ramsden, who won the country’s largest electorate, Te Tai Tonga.

So, hundreds of thousands of special votes are going to potentially change where things stand right now.

In his speech tonight, Luxon said 300,000 special votes were yet to be counted. Those votes, which will be counted and known by 27 November, typically sway left.

In the 2023 election there 600,000 special votes, and National dropped from 50 seats in the preliminary count to 48 seats.

In a 130-seat parliament, in which 66 seats are needed to govern, this is where things stand with 97% of the vote counted. However, special votes are still to be counted and the lay of the land will not be clear until 27 November.

Labour 35, Greens 16, Te Pāti Māori 5 = 56

National 29.3%, Labour 29.2%, Greens 13.3%, Act 11%, NZ First 9.8%, Opportunity 6%, Te Pāti Māori 0.6%.

Jamie Tahana has this hot-take on the Luxon speech:

It was a tough line to tread for a speech: National is the biggest party on current results – 29.30% – but Luxon has also led the party to one of its most unpopular points, only 0.1% ahead of Labour. Luxon has also lost some of his most senior and experienced MPs, which he conceded required “some tough phone calls”.

Luxon said that because of the closeness of the result, and with special votes still to be counted, caretaker conventions will apply.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

Caretaker convention basically means that while a new government is being formed, the incumbent government is in “caretaker” mode, which means it will carry on the normal business of governing.

Jamie Tahana notes that for Luxon to continue as prime minister, he will have to cobble together a three- or four-party coalition, from a weakened position compared to 2023.

In his speech, Luxon said: “Negotiations and compromises are going to be required … A number of races are still too close to call.”

Jamie Tahana has some more from the Luxon speech:

Luxon called the election “too close to call”, and said “meaningful negotiations” for a new coalition government will start after the final results are known on 27 November.

“It looks like an MMP special,” he said. “It’s heartening to see that so far National is once again the largest party in parliament and we have a path to forming the next government.”

Luxon concedes result unlikely tonight, saying that meaningful negotiations will begin after final results on 27 November.

Luxon arrived to a crowd of about 700 raucous supporters chanting “three more years”. He was flanked by his wife Amanda and two children, waved and grinned as he descended a stairwell flanked in blue light.

A heavy music track - which could not be immediately identified through Shazam - thrummed as he walked down a long aisle, hugging supporters.

When he took the podium, Luxon conceded the election was “too close to call” but the crowd cheered as he announced National had secured the highest party count.

He went on to acknowledge Labour leader Chris Hipkins, and admonished supporters who booed Quilae Wong and Opportunity, saying it was “not the kiwi way”.

Luxon is also paying tribute to those high-ranking National MPs who find themselves out of a job tonight: Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith and Gerry Brownlee. “I want to thank you … for your years of service to our party”.

Luxon has called tonight’s result “an MMP special” and says “on the numbers so far, National is once again the largest party in parliament”.

It will be interesting to see what Luxon has to say. How will he frame the vote count and National’s performance?

Christopher Luxon is high-fiving and hugging supporters. He’ll be speaking soon.

#67. Surgeon having lunch near Krakow shooting undoubtedly saved officer's life, colleague says

Watch: Doctor who treated victims of Krakow shooting speaks to the BBC

One of two surgeons who treated victims on the streets in the aftermath of a mass shooting in Krakow, Poland said his colleague "undoubtedly" saved the life of a gravely injured police officer.

The pair were eating near the city's Main Market Square on Saturday when a gunman began firing at random, killing six people and injuring 15.

The doctors raced to treat both the attacker and two officers shot while subduing the perpetrator.

On Sunday, the officers - both hailed as "true heroes" by the prime minister for preventing further deaths - emerged from comas.

Prosecutors launched a terror investigation after ministers previously said the 24-year-old Polish attacker - who remains in a critical condition - was apparently inspired by "far-right ideologies".

A British national and Austrian woman were among those killed alongside four Poles.

Professor Michal PÄdziwiatr was having lunch with a colleague, named by local media as Dr Mateusz Wierdak, and their families in central Krakow when they heard gunshots.

PÄdziwiatr told the BBC: "We saw the shooting attack. And what a surgeon can do in a situation like this?... We decided to help the victims as soon as possible.

"We rushed into the area where the shooting was and started our rescue, because there were no ambulances available, there were no professionals around."

What we know about the deadly Krakow shooting

He said his colleague used his hand to stem the bleeding from the most injured policeman, allowing them to wait for an ambulance which transferred the officer to hospital.

PÄdziwiatr said they came to learn that one of those they treated was the apparent attacker.

"As a doctor... it doesn't matter to me if it's a victim or a suspect," he said.

"It's not our task to decide what to do with this guy... for us, it was another heavily injured victim, that's all."

According to local media, Wierdak is a renowned surgeon in Krakow. The University Hospital in Krakow states PÄdziwiatr is head of General, Oncological, Metabolic and Emergency Surgery.

The police officers have been identified as senior constables Jakub Kulik and Artur MoraÅski.

The attacker has not yet been named by authorities.

When his house was searched after the attack in central Krakow, the bodies of his parents and sister were found.

The National Public Prosecutor's Office in Poland launched an investigation into the attack with a unit specialising in "terrorist offences" taking the lead.

Separately, reports emerged that six Dublin Civil Defence volunteers also assisted in the aftermath of the shooting.

Investigations continued in central Krakow on Sunday before Main Market Square reopened

James McConnell, an officer, told RTE News the group were on a city break and were close to the main square, external when the shooting happened.

The volunteers who are trained to provide medical care immediately sought to help injured people, providing "CPR and haemorrhage control".

Witnesses who were close to the attack described panic and confusion around the square as it unfolded.

Ruby Catherine Warren, from London, heard gunfire and "yelled at my friends to run and we all sprinted for our lives".

Not knowing where the gunman was - or if there was more than one - they "just kept sprinting", eventually finding a hotel to hide inside.

How the deadly shooting in Krakow unfolded

Tom Brook, from West Yorkshire, was inside a McDonald's near the square when he heard "multiple gunshots and saw people running for cover".

"I then walked into the square and saw paramedics working on someone outside [a shop] and there was a dead body covered in foil blankets 20 meters away," he said.

The suspect wore full military tactical gear during the attack and was described as having a military-grade weapon - but the government said he was not a member of the Polish armed forces.

Poland's Interior Minister Marcin KierwiÅski said "everything suggests" the suspect was "fascinated by far-right ideologies" and held ultra-nationalist views.

The rampage began shortly before 15:00 local time (14:00 GMT) on Saturday in the historic centre of Krakow.

Footage showed the attacker's vehicle speeding through the centre of the city, and people fleeing as the sound of gunfire rang out.

The glass fronts of shops were shattered by bullets and a tram was shot at during the attack, which police say lasted for around three minutes.

The mayor of Krakow said he had submitted an urgent request for the two officers injured while stopping the attacker to be awarded titles of honorary citizens of the city.

Main Market Square reopened on Sunday evening after a security cordon was lifted.

#62. NZ election 2026: New Zealand faces weeks of uncertainty with results ‘too close to call’

National party will be largest in parliament but ultimate shape of next government won’t be settled for some time

New Zealand could be facing a weeks-long wait to know the shape of its next government, after its nail-biting election failed to hand a decisive victory to either the incumbent rightwing coalition or opposition left bloc.

On preliminary results, the centre-right National party, led by the prime minister, Christopher Luxon, managed to secure enough seats to govern alongside its traditional coalition partners, the libertarian Act and populist New Zealand First parties.

But with some seats won by very slim margins, and special votes yet to be counted, that success is precarious, resulting in an election too close to call until official results are released on 27 November.

Special votes include overseas votes and ballots cast outside a voter’s electorate. In 2023, 600,000 such votes were made, 21% of the total. These votes tend to favour the left – in 2023, special votes resulted in Te Pāti Māori (the Māori party) and the Greens gaining three seats between them.

With nearly 100% of the regular votes counted on Saturday night, National secured 42 seats, Act 13 and New Zealand First 12. The combined 67-seat majority is wafer thin, with the left bloc just three seats behind.

Speaking to a room full of the party faithful at Auckland’s Viaduct events centre just before midnight, Luxon said the results were “too close to call” and the special votes would need to be counted before negotiations could begin.

National was still the largest party in parliament, he said, and had “a pathway to forming the next government”. National pipped Labour by 0.02% in the party vote.

Labour, led by Chris Hipkins, secured 35 seats, while its traditional coalition partners the Greens won 16 and Te Pāti Māori five. Even with the seven seats from the centrist newcomer party, Opportunity, the left bloc is three seats short of forming a government.

Before a rowdy crowd of supporters at the Cosmopolitan Club in Upper Hutt, north of Wellington, Hipkins joked that New Zealand had spoken but “it may take a bit longer to work out what they’ve said”.

“As things stand tonight, it is clear that we are not going to know the shape of our next government for a little while longer yet,” Hipkins said, promising that Labour would get “straight to work” should the final count land in its favour.

The collapse of the two major parties, amid a sluggish economy and stubbornly high ⁠inflation, is feeding record support for the smaller parties, including Act, New Zealand First and the Greens. It is also helped propel Opportunity into parliament for the first time.

Opinion polls in the lead-up to the election had Opportunity placed to become the kingmaker. At this stage, the right bloc does not need the party to govern, but if special votes change the landscape, its predicted role could come to fruition.

Currently at 5.9%, the centrist party has met the threshold needed to enter parliament, a historic first. Since New Zealand adopted the mixed-member proportional (MMP) electoral system in 1996, no new party has been able to meet that threshold – all the other minor parties have been born from MPs or groups splintering from larger parties.

The party’s leader, Qiulae Wong, told her supporters in Auckland she was “stoked”.

“It’s looking very promising for us,” she said. “Let’s just see how the rest of the votes fall.”

In New Zealand, voters cast two votes: one for an electorate MP and one for a party. The system is meant to produce a parliament that reflects each party’s share of the nationwide vote.

However, if New Zealanders vote for more electorate MPs from a particular party than their party vote would entitle them to, they get to keep the extra MPs in what is called an overhang.

National won more seats that its party support allows for, meaning it could gain an extra five seats to make up the balance.

That will be good news for its path to power, but comes with some significant trade-offs for the party. Four of its most senior ministers, including Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith, Chris Bishop and Gerry Brownlee, will not return to parliament because they did not win a seat, or the party didn’t win enough votes to bring in list-only MPs.

On the other side of the house, Te Pāti Māori picked up five overhang seats.

With all the extra seats, there could be 130 in the next parliament – a record under MMP – which means 65 seats are needed to form a coalition.

The country faces a two-week wait until special votes are announced, followed by the possibility of weeks of negotiations. Luxon said caretaker government conventions would apply in the meantime.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well-established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

#60. 'Devastating message' as ANC records worst-ever result in South Africa election

Voters cast their ballots at a polling station during South Africa's local government elections in Johannesburg

South Africa's ANC has recorded its worst-ever showing in local elections since Nelson Mandela led the country out of apartheid and into democracy more than 30 years ago, a result the party has called "catastrophic".

While it won the most votes of all parties in the country, the African National Congress won 31% of the overall vote, one third down from the 46% it gained in 2021, and a far cry from the 65% 20 years ago.

The story of its decline is also clear in the provinces and cities. The party won a majority in just one of the country's nine provinces, Limpopo. In 2021 it won six. It also won the most votes in just two of the eight cities, compared with six at the last local elections.

The Democratic Alliance (DA) came second in the overall vote with 25%, retaining majority control of the city of Cape Town. Former president Jacob Zuma's MK party came third overall with 10%, unseating the ANC as winner in the city of Durban, while the Economic Freedom Fighters (EFF), led by Julius Malema, once seen as a serious challenge to the ANC, came fourth with 6%.

"This has been a very catastrophic outcome for the ANC," said secretary-general Fikile Mbalula on Saturday. "We knew this disaster is coming, we're staring at it. As we got into elections, the cost of living of our people was too high. The middle class have turned on the ANC in big numbers."

Entering the count centre on Sunday nigh, South Africa's president and ANC leader Cyril Ramaphosa said: "We accept the will of the people however concerning it might be, we accept that this is how the people of South Africa in our towns, in our cities, in our metros have spoken - and this is their verdict, we accept it with humility. It is a decline that is concerning, that is how I characterise it."

President Cyril Ramaphosa is due to be replaced as ANC leader next year

It's a continuing decline for the ANC, which lost its parliamentary majority in the national elections in 2024 for the first time since the end of apartheid in 1994. It then had to form a coalition government with a raft of other parties in what is called the Government of National Unity (GNU).

While the Democratic Alliance made significant gains in the cities in these local elections, its overall vote share rose only marginally from 22% in 2021, and was below the 27% it gained in 2016.

Voter turnout was 52%, an increase from 46% in 2021, when elections were held during the Covid pandemic.

"This election is delivering a devastating message to the ANC," said political analyst Susan Booysen. "It's an ANC in decline at local municipal level, at the metropolitan level, and when the results are reflected provincially and nationally, we see severe declines in ANC levels of support all around."

The reason for that decline in support is largely due to poor service delivery and infrastructure across the country, from water and power to sanitation. "These are huge problems," says Booysen, "and have really been affecting the lives of ordinary people, in the suburbs, in the townships, and in very dramatic ways."

The big picture looks even more bleak for the ANC. The party will elect a new leader next year as Ramaphosa comes to end of his term. He is due to stay on as president of the country until the next national elections in 2029, but there are few candidates lined up to succeed him in either role.

ANC under pressure to reverse South Africa decline

"It is sort of the end of the liberation movement era in South Africa," said political analyst William Gumede, "and the dominance of the liberation movement since the end of apartheid. So the ANC is going to be a party that by 2029 could be the second biggest rather than the biggest party. It may actually spiral into a long period of just total infighting. This is a new era for South Africa's politics and also the ANC."

The elections also saw a significant shift in voting patterns, with the DA, traditionally known as the "white party" for attracting a largely white vote, gaining more black votes. "We're moving towards a post-racial voting pattern in South Africa," added Gumede.

South Africa now faces days of local coalition building, with the three metropolitan municipalities in the province of Gauteng, the country's powerhouse, under particular scrutiny. The ANC may even find itself shut out in the cities of Johannesburg and Pretoria.

"We are now in a coalition country," said Gumede. "Essentially, what the voters are saying is: 'Well, the ANC has dominated the last three decades, they didn't use their power to change the society for the good, so we don't want the dominant parties anymore, whether at national level, provincial level, or rather now at a local level. We actually want coalitions. We want people to work together.'"

Parties now have 14 days from the moment results were announced to negotiate coalitions.

#57. Uyghurs facing deportation by Thailand say they will be ‘like slaves’ in China

Activists fear Bangkok will give in to pressure from Beijing despite reports of torture and human rights abuses against the mostly muslim ethnic minority

The deportation of two more Uyghurs to China from Thailand would put their lives in grave danger, activists have said, with one of the men saying he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back.

Last year, Thai authorities forcibly deported 40 Uyghur men to China despite pleas from the men themselves and the UN. The US and Canada had reportedly offered to resettle them, but Bangkok appeared to want to avoid upsetting Beijing. The UN has said the returnees’ whereabouts remain unknown.

Now, two more men fear becoming the latest victims of Beijing’s repressive crackdown against the largely Muslim ethnic group, condemned by the UN as a potential crime against humanity.

The girlfriend of one of the men said her boyfriend, whose name is being withheld for security reasons, told the Guardian he wanted to go “anywhere else but China”.

Human rights groups said two Uyghur men were at risk of deportation after recently being transferred to an immigration detention centre and they feared Thai authorities may once again give in to pressure from Beijing.

Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch, called for the men to be allowed to travel to a safe third country. “Previous Thai governments have kept Uyghurs in inhumane detention for years and then complied with Beijing’s demands to send them to China, where they were forcibly disappeared,” she said.

The men had been serving a seven-year prison sentence for offences related to an attempted escape from immigration detention in January 2020. They were part of a group of more than 200 Uyghur men, women and children who were apprehended by Thai police in 2014 near the border with Malaysia after they fled from China.

Since 2014, women and children in the group have been released to Turkey, while the men have been deported to China, in separate batches, attracting criticism from the United Nations and the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio.

Uyghurs, a Turkic minority mainly from the Xinjiang region in China, have been subjected to decades of oppressive and discriminatory treatment from the Chinese authorities. This has included the incarceration of hundreds of thousands of people in detention camps aimed at stamping out their cultural identity.

Beijing has defended its policies in Xinjiang as being aimed at poverty alleviation and counter-terrorism.

In July, Marzeyya Kasem’s (who asked to be referred to by her Islamic alias) boyfriend wrote to her in a letter that he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back to China. “Sometimes I thought why I’m a human being. What I do wrong against China … no want to go back to China,” he wrote. She shared the letter with a Thai rights group.

The two men facing imminent deportation reportedly pleaded their case to Thailand’s office of the National Human Rights Commission on Wednesday.

Relatives of Uyghurs have said the Chinese government was “paranoid about Uyghurs going to a safe country in large numbers and living together”, and preferred to have them deported.

It is not clear whether China has issued a formal extradition request for the two men. But Chaipruak Doungprapat, Thailand’s national security council chief, told reporters that the men, along with three other Uyghurs who are still in prison, had arrest warrants outstanding in China.

Some have questioned whether criminal warrants could be credible considering that at least one of them, Kasem’s boyfriend, was a minor when he left China. “If they are really wanted for a crime in China, why didn’t China immediately issue a [Interpol] red notice [when they were detained in 2014]?” asked Kasem.

Kasem believes that because Thailand was criticised for last year’s mass deportation, this time the authorities were trying to establish a more credible legal basis for the move.

A spokesperson for China’s foreign affairs ministry said: “The cooperation between China and Thailand in combating cross-border illegal and criminal activities such as smuggling is consistent with the laws of the two countries, international law and international practice.”

A spokesperson for Thailand’s foreign affairs ministry declined to comment.

#55. Seven dead and more than 100 injured after trains collide in Egypt

Footage shows derailed train carriages near Giza in Egypt

At least seven people have died in a train crash near the Egyptian city of Giza, with more than 100 injured.

The country's railway authority said two trains travelling south from Giza and Cairo collided, causing several carriages to derail and fall down an embankment.

Images from the scene showed people looking inside overturned carriages for injured passengers as a massive emergency response was launched.

Egyptian President Abdul Fattah al-Sisi said he had ordered state authorities to "investigate the circumstances of the accident, and hold accountable anyone found to have been negligent".

The public prosecutor's office warned the death toll could rise.

Uninjured passengers and bystanders searched derailed carriages for injured people

The government said hospitals in the area had been put "on heightened alert" as they dealt with a massive influx of patients.

Egypt's health minister Dr Khaled Abdel Ghaffar said people were being treated for broken bones and head injuries.

The Egyptian Red Crescent said it was providing aid and psychological support to victims and their families at the scene.

The driver of one of the trains was among those who died in the crash, the ministry confirmed.

The first train involved in the crash was travelling from Giza to Fayoum, south of Cairo, when it crashed into the rear of another service heading south from Cairo to Aswan, which was stationary on the tracks.

Images from the scene showed some carriages had overturned, with the body of at least one appearing to have split in two.

Trains between Giza and the Upper Egypt region were suspended as cranes were dispatched to remove the wrecked trains. The railway authority has not said how long it expects this to take.

The collision occurred in the area between Kafr Ammar and Al-Raqqa stations in Al-Ayyat, close to where Egypt's deadliest train tragedy occurred in February 2002, when a fire engulfed a passenger train travelling from Cairo to Luxor.

In that incident, more than 370 people were officially reported killed, although some estimates put the toll considerably higher.

Two further crashes in the area in 2009 killed dozens more.

There were calls in Egypt on Sunday for the transport minister to resign over the latest deadly rail disaster.

Foreign leaders and dignitaries have shared condolences and support for the victims.

Saudi Arabia's ministry of foreign affairs expressed "solidarity" with Egypt and wished those hurt a speedy recovery.

Turkey's ambassador to Egypt wrote on X that he extended "heartfelt condolences and sincere sympathy" to the victims, their families, and the Egyptian people.
